\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times\frac{\Gamma_{q}(-z)}{\Gamma_{q}(\theta N-z)},

(3.18)

where the product in (
3.17
) is over
1\leq i\leq N-1
and
\lambda_{i+1}\leq j<\lambda_{i}
.
The term (
3.17
) is of the form
P(q^{-z})/Q(q^{-z})
, where
P,Q\in\mathbb{C}(q,q^{\theta})[x]
are both polynomials of degree
\lambda_{1}
. It follows that there exist constants
M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime}>0
such that
\Re z>M_{2}^{\prime}
implies
|P(q^{-z})|/|Q(q^{-z})|<M_{1}^{\prime}
.

Thus we are left to deal with (
3.18
), which by definition of the
q
-Gamma function equals
(1-q)^{\theta N}\frac{(q^{\theta N-z};q)_{\infty}}{(q^{-z};q)_{\infty}}
. For any
\theta
with
\Re\theta>0
, we have
|(1-q)^{\theta}|<1
. Thus we only need to prove the existence of
M_{1}^{\prime\prime},M_{2}^{\prime\prime}>0
such that
z\in\mathcal{C}^{+}
,
\Re z>M_{2}^{\prime\prime}
, implies

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\big(q^{\theta N-z};q\big)_{\infty}}{(q^{-z};q)_{\infty}}\right|<M_{1}^{\prime\prime},\qquad\textrm{for all}\quad\theta\in K.

(3.19)

There exists
a>0
large enough such that

\displaystyle q^{-a}\big(1-q^{N\inf_{\theta\in K}{\Re\theta}}\big)>2.

(3.20)

Note that such
a
exists because
K\subset\{\theta\in\mathbb{C}\colon\Re\theta>0\}
is compact and so
\inf_{\theta\in K}{\Re\theta}>0
. Now consider only
z\in\mathcal{C}^{+}
with
\Re z>a+1
, and let
M=M(z)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\lfloor\Re z-a\rfloor\in\mathbb{N}
. From the triangle inequality,
|1-q^{\theta N-z+j}|\leq 1+q^{N\Re\theta-\Re z+j}
and
|1-q^{-z+j}|\geq q^{-\Re z+j}-1
. Along with condition (
3.20
), we deduce that
|1-q^{-z+j}|\geq|1-q^{\theta N-z+j}|
, for all
0\leq j\leq M
. Since we can write

\displaystyle\frac{\big(q^{\theta N-z};q\big)_{\infty}}{(q^{-z};q)_{\infty}}=\frac{\big(1-q^{\theta N-z}\big)\cdots\big(1-q^{\theta N-z+M}\big)}{(1-q^{-z})\cdots\big(1-q^{-z+M}\big)}\cdot\frac{\big(q^{\theta N-z+M+1};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{-z+M+1};q\big)_{\infty}},

it follows that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\big(q^{\theta N-z};q\big)_{\infty}}{(q^{-z};q)_{\infty}}\right|\leq\left|\frac{\big(q^{\theta N-z+M+1};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{-z+M+1};q\big)_{\infty}}\right|.

(3.21)

Thus we only need to show that the right-hand side of (
3.21
) is bounded by a constant, for all
z\in\mathcal{C}^{+}
with
\Re z
large enough.

Since
M=\lfloor\Re z-a\rfloor>\Re z-a-1
, we have
|q^{-z+M+1}|=q^{M+1-\Re z}\leq q^{-a}
and
|q^{\theta N-z+M+1}|\leq q^{N\Re\theta-a}<q^{-a}
. Moreover, for
z\in\mathcal{C}^{+}
with
\Re z
large enough,
|\Im q^{-z+M+1}|\geq q^{-a+1}|\sin(\ln{q}\cdot\Im z)|\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}m_{1}(z)
and since
|\Im z|
is a constant between
0
and
-\frac{\pi}{2\ln{q}}
for
z\in\mathcal{C}^{+}
with
\Re z
large enough, then
m_{1}(z)=m_{1}>0
is a strictly positive constant independent of
z\in\mathcal{C}^{+}
as long as
\Re z
is large enough. Since the function
(x;q)_{\infty}
is continuous on
x\in\mathbb{C}
, we have
c_{\max}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\sup\limits_{|x|\leq q^{-a}}{|(x;q)_{\infty}|}<\infty
, and
c_{\min}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\inf_{|x|\leq q^{-a},|\Im x|\geq m_{1}}{|(x;q)_{\infty}|}\in(0,\infty)
. Thus the right-hand side of (
3.21
) is upper bounded by the constant
c_{\max}/c_{\min}<\infty
.
∎

Proof of Step 4.
We prove a stronger statement than step 4. Let
M>0
be
any
positive number. We show that both sides of (
3.2
) are uniformly bounded on
\{\theta\in\mathbb{C}\colon\Re\theta\geq M\}
. Let us begin with the left-hand side of (
3.2
). Observe that
\theta
appears in the left side only within the variable
t=q^{\theta}
and
|t|=q^{\Re\theta}\leq q^{M}
. Name
\epsilon=q^{M}\in(0,1)
; we have to prove that there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{|t|\leq\epsilon}{\left|\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(xt^{N-1},t^{N-2},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(t^{N-1},t^{N-2},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}\right|}\leq C.

Thanks to the branching rule for Macdonald polynomials, Theorem
2.5
, and the assumptions
|x|\leq 1
,
x\neq 0
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(xt^{N-1},t^{N-2},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(t^{N-1},t^{N-2},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}\right|\leq\sum_{\mu\prec\lambda}{|\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)||t|^{(N-1)(|\lambda|-|\mu|)}\left|\frac{P_{\mu}\big(t^{N-2},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}\right|}.

Given
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
, there are finitely many
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N-1}
with
\mu\prec\lambda
. Thus it suffices to prove that there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle|\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)|\leq C_{1},\qquad\left|t^{(N-1)(|\lambda|-|\mu|)}\frac{P_{\mu}\big(t^{N-2},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}{P_{\mu}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}\right|\leq C_{2},

where
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
do not depend on
t
, though they may depend on
\mu
.

The branching coefficient
|\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)|
, due to the expression in Theorem
2.5
, is a finite product of terms of the form
\frac{1-q^{a}t^{b}}{1-q^{c}t^{d}}
, with
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
(c,d)\neq(0,0)
. We have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1-q^{a}t^{b}}{1-q^{c}t^{d}}\right|\leq 2\big(1-q^{c}\epsilon^{d}\big)^{-1}

for all
|t|\leq\epsilon
and
2(1-q^{c}\epsilon^{d})^{-1}
does not depend on
t
, so the boundedness of
|\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)|
follows.

Due to the evaluation identity for Macdonald polynomials, Theorem
2.3
, we have

\displaystyle t^{(N-1)(|\lambda|-|\mu|)}\frac{P_{\mu}\big(t^{N-2},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}=t^{(N-1)(|\lambda|-|\mu|)+n(\mu)-n(\lambda)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\times\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N-1}{\frac{(q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{j-i};q)_{\infty}(t^{j-i+1};q)_{\infty}}{(t^{j-i};q)_{\infty}(q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{j-i+1};q)_{\infty}}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}{\frac{(t^{j-i};q)_{\infty}(q^{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}t^{j-i+1};q)_{\infty}}{(q^{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}t^{j-i};q)_{\infty}(t^{j-i+1};q)_{\infty}}},

where
n(\lambda)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}(N-1)\lambda_{N}+(N-2)\lambda_{N-1}+\dots+2\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{2}
and similarly for
n(\mu)
.

The last two terms above are products of a finite number of fractions
\frac{1-q^{a}t^{b}}{1-q^{c}t^{d}}
, with
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
(c,d)\neq(0,0)
, and as we saw above it is implied that the absolute value of the last two terms above are upper bounded by a constant independent of
t
(as long as
|t|\leq\epsilon
). Thus our only goal is to show there is an upper bound for
t^{(N-1)(|\lambda|-|\mu|)+n(\mu)-n(\lambda)}
; this fact follows if the exponent is nonnegative. In fact, we have

\displaystyle(N-1)(|\lambda|-|\mu|)-(n(\lambda)-n(\mu))

\displaystyle\qquad{}=(N-1)(|\lambda|-|\mu|)-((N-1)\lambda_{N}+(N-2)(\lambda_{N-1}-\mu_{N-1})+\dots+(\lambda_{2}-\mu_{2}))

\displaystyle\qquad{}\geq(N-1)(|\lambda|-|\mu|)-(N-1)(\lambda_{N}+\lambda_{N-1}-\mu_{N-1}+\dots+\lambda_{2}-\mu_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad{}=(N-1)(\lambda_{1}-\mu_{1})\geq 0.

Let us proceed to prove uniform boundedness of the right-hand side of (
3.2
) on
\{\theta\in\mathbb{C}\colon\Re\theta\geq M\}
. First of all, the triangular inequality gives

\displaystyle\big|\big(xt^{N};q\big)_{\infty}\big|\leq\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}{\big(1+|x|q^{N\Re\theta+i}\big)}\leq\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}{\big(1+|x|q^{i}\big)},

\displaystyle|(xq;q)_{\infty}|=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}{\big|\big(1-xq^{i}\big)\big|}\geq\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}{\big(1-|x|q^{i}\big)},

so the factor
\big(xt^{N};q\big)_{\infty}/(xq;q)_{\infty}
in (
3.2
) has an upper-bounded absolute value. We are left to deal with

\displaystyle\Gamma_{q}(\theta N)\int_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}{x^{z}\prod_{i=1}^{N}{\frac{\Gamma_{q}(\lambda_{i}+\theta(N-i)-z)}{\Gamma_{q}(\lambda_{i}+\theta(N-i+1)-z)}}{\rm d}z}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\int_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}{x^{z}\Gamma_{q}(\theta N)\prod_{i=1}^{N}{\frac{\Gamma_{q}(\lambda_{i}+\theta(N-i)-z)}{\Gamma_{q}(\lambda_{i}+\theta(N-i+1)-z)}}{\rm d}z}

(3.22)

and prove its absolute value is uniformly bounded on
\{\theta\in\mathbb{C}\colon\Re\theta\geq M\}
.

For any
M_{2}>0
, the contribution of the portion
\mathcal{C}^{+}\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\colon\Re z\leq M_{2}\}
of the contour is bounded by a constant. In fact,
\Re\theta\geq M
implies
|t|=|q^{\theta}|=q^{\Re\theta}\leq q^{M}
and
\theta
appears in the integrand of (
3.2
) only as part of the exponent of some
q
, thus the integrand can be written as a function of
z
and
t
(with
q\in(0,1)
fixed). Thus for
(z,t)
in the compact subset
(\mathcal{C}^{+}\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\colon\Re z\leq M_{2}\})\times\big[0,q^{M}\big]
, the integrand in (
3.22
) attains a maximum value
L<\infty
, and the contribution of the integral in the portion
\mathcal{C}^{+}\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\colon\Re z\leq M_{2}\}
of the contour is upper bounded by
L
times the length of that finite portion.

Since
|x|<1
, the term
x^{z}
decreases exponentially as
|z|\rightarrow\infty
,
z\in\mathcal{C}^{+}
. Thus to deal with the infinite portion of the integral
\mathcal{C}^{+}\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\colon\Re z\geq M_{2}\}
, it is enough to show that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{q}(\theta N)\prod_{i=1}^{N}{\frac{\Gamma_{q}(\lambda_{i}+\theta(N-i)-z)}{\Gamma_{q}(\lambda_{i}+\theta(N-i+1)-z)}}=(1-q)\frac{(q;q)_{\infty}}{(q^{\theta N};q)_{\infty}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}{\frac{\big(q^{\lambda_{i}+\theta(N-i+1)-z};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{\lambda_{i}+\theta(N-i)-z};q\big)_{\infty}}}

has bounded absolute value for all
z\in\mathcal{C}^{+}
,
\Re z>M_{2}
and
\theta\in\mathbb{C}
,
\Re\theta\geq M
, for a suitable constant
M_{2}>0
. Clearly
\big|\big(q^{\theta N};q\big)\big|^{-1}\leq\big(q^{N\Re\theta};q\big)^{-1}\leq\big(q^{NM};q\big)^{-1}
, thus we only need a bound on the absolute value of

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}{\frac{\big(q^{\lambda_{i}+\theta(N-i+1)-z};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{\lambda_{i}+\theta(N-i)-z};q\big)_{\infty}}},

(3.23)

uniformly over all
z\in\mathcal{C}^{+}
,
\Re z>M_{2}
, and
\theta\in\mathbb{C}
,
\Re\theta\geq M
. We can bound the absolute value of (
3.23
) by

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}{\left|\frac{\big(q^{\lambda_{i+1}+\theta(N-i)-z};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{\lambda_{i}+\theta(N-i)-z};q\big)_{\infty}}\right|}\times\left|\frac{\big(q^{\lambda_{1}+\theta N-z};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{\lambda_{N}-z};q\big)_{\infty}}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq N-1\\
\lambda_{i+1}\leq j<\lambda_{i}\end{subarray}}{\big|1-q^{\theta(N-i)+j-z}\big|}\times\frac{1}{\prod\limits_{\lambda_{N}\leq j<\lambda_{1}}{|1-q^{j-z}|}}\times\left|\frac{\big(q^{\lambda_{1}+\theta N-z};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{\lambda_{1}-z};q\big)_{\infty}}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq N-1\\
\lambda_{i+1}\leq j<\lambda_{i}\end{subarray}}{\big(1+q^{(N-i)\Re\theta+j-\Re z}\big)}\times\frac{1}{\prod\limits_{\lambda_{N}\leq j<\lambda_{1}}{\big(q^{j-\Re z}-1\big)}}\times\left|\frac{\big(q^{\lambda_{1}+\theta N-z};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{\lambda_{1}-z};q\big)_{\infty}}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leq\prod_{\lambda_{N}\leq j<\lambda_{1}}{\frac{1+q^{j-\Re z}}{q^{j-\Re z}-1}}\times\left|\frac{\big(q^{\lambda_{1}+\theta N-z};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{\lambda_{1}-z};q\big)_{\infty}}\right|.

If
\Re z
is large enough, the product
\prod\limits_{\lambda_{N}\leq j<\lambda_{1}}{\frac{1+q^{j-\Re z}}{q^{j-\Re z}-1}}
is clearly upper bounded by a constant. We still have to bound
\left|\frac{(q^{\lambda_{1}+\theta N-z};q)_{\infty}}{(q^{\lambda_{1}-z};q)_{\infty}}\right|
. Since
\lambda_{1}
is real and
\Im z
is constant for
z\in\mathcal{C}^{+}
,
\Re z
large enough, it suffices to prove the following statement: there exist constants
M_{1},M_{2}>0
such that
z\in\mathcal{C}^{+}
,
\Re z>M_{2}
, and
\Re\theta\geq M
imply

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\big(q^{\theta N-z};q\big)_{\infty}}{(q^{-z};q)_{\infty}}\right|<M_{1}.

This statement was proved above in step 3, see (
3.19
). In that case,
\theta
varied over a compact subset
K\subset\{\theta\in\mathbb{C}\colon\Re\theta>0\}
, but in this case
\theta
varies over a closed infinite domain of the form
\{\theta\in\mathbb{C}\colon\Re\theta\geq M\}
. However, the expression
\frac{(q^{\theta N-z};q)_{\infty}}{(q^{-z};q)_{\infty}}
depends on
\theta
only by means of
q^{\theta}
, so the proof of (
3.19
) above can be repeated word-by-word, since we only used
|t|\leq q^{\inf_{\theta\in K}{\Re\theta}}<1
in that proof.

4
Multiplicative formulas for Macdonald characters

We now come to the multiplicative formulas. All of our results require parameter
\theta
to be a positive integer. In this section,
q
is typically a variable (but of course, we can specialize
q
to a complex number later).

4.1
Statement of the multiplicative theorem and some consequences

We need some non-standard terminology on
q
-difference operators. The
q
-shift operators
\{T_{q,x_{i}}\colon i=1,\dots,m\}
are linear operators on
\mathbb{C}(q)[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
that act as

\displaystyle(T_{q,x_{i}}f)(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}f(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},qx_{i},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{m}).

The
q
-degree operators
\{D_{q,x_{i}}\}_{i=1,\dots,m}
are linear operators on
\mathbb{C}(q)[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
defined by

\displaystyle D_{q,x_{i}}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\frac{T_{q,x_{i}}-1}{q-1}\qquad\text{or}

\displaystyle(D_{q,x_{i}}f)(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=\frac{f(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},qx_{i},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{m})-f(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})}{q-1}.

The
q
-difference operators that appear in the multiplicative formulas for Macdonald polynomials are finite sums of terms

\displaystyle c_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q)T_{q,x_{1}}^{i_{1}}\cdots T_{q,x_{m}}^{i_{m}}\qquad\text{or}\qquad c_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q)D_{q,x_{1}}^{i_{1}}\cdots D_{q,x_{m}}^{i_{m}},

where
(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
vary over a finite subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m}
, and
c_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q)
are rational functions in the variables
q,x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
. Thus the operators that we consider are linear operators
\mathbb{C}(q)[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}(q,x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
that act on polynomials and yield rational functions.

Recall the setting of Jacobi Trudi’s formula for Macdonald polynomials, Theorem
2.7
. The expressions
C_{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{n}}^{(q,q^{\theta})}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
were defined in (
2.5
); they are rational functions in
q,u_{1},\dots,u_{n}
whose denominators are products of linear factors. We define
M_{\theta}^{(m)}
as the set of strictly upper-triangular
m\times m
matrices whose entries belong to
\{0,1,\dots,\theta\}
(the cardinality of
M_{\theta}^{(m)}
is
(\theta+1){m\choose 2}
). For any strictly upper-triangular
m\times m
matrix
\tau
and
1\leq i\leq m
, let
\tau_{i}^{+}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\sum\limits_{j=i+1}^{m}{\tau_{i,j}}
(resp.
\tau_{i}^{-}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{i-1}{\tau_{j,i}}
) be the sum of the entries of
\tau
to the right of
(i,i)
(resp. sum of entries of
\tau
above
(i,i)
), cf. (
2.6
). The main theorem of this section is the following.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\theta\in\mathbb{N}
,
t=q^{\theta}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
. Then

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};N,q,q^{\theta}\big)=\frac{q^{\theta^{2}{m+1\choose 3}-\left\{N\theta^{2}-{\theta+1\choose 2}\right\}{m\choose 2}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}{[\theta(N-i+1)-1]_{q}!}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta(N-m+1)-1}{(x_{i}-q^{j-\theta})}}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\times\frac{1}{\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq m\\
0\leq k<\theta\end{subarray}}{(x_{i}-q^{k}x_{j})}}\times\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{q,\theta}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(x_{i};N,q,q^{\theta}\big)\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\big(x_{i}-q^{j-\theta}\big)}}{[\theta N-1]_{q}!}}\right\},

(4.1)

where
\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{q,\theta}
is the
q
-difference operator
\mathbb{C}(q)[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}(q,x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{q,\theta}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\frac{1}{(q-1)^{\theta{m\choose 2}}}\sum_{\tau\in M^{(m)}_{\theta}}\left\{C_{\tau}^{(q,q^{\theta})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\prod_{i=1}^{m}{T_{q,x_{i}}^{(i-1)\theta+\tau_{i}^{+}-\tau_{i}^{-}}}\right\},

(4.2)

where for any
\tau\in M_{\theta}^{(m)}
, we denoted

\displaystyle C_{\tau}^{(q,q^{\theta})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})

\displaystyle\qquad{}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\prod_{s=1}^{m-1}{C_{\tau_{1,s+1},\dots,\tau_{s,s+1}}^{(q,q^{\theta})}\left(\{u_{i}=x_{s+1}^{-1}x_{i}q^{-\theta+\sum\limits_{j=s+2}^{m}{(\tau_{i,j}-\tau_{s+1,j})}}\colon 1\leq i\leq s\}\right)}.

(4.3)

The proof of Theorem
4.1
is given in the next subsection. We derive here some conclusions, namely two special cases when the operator
\mathcal{D}_{q,\theta}^{(m)}
has a simple form. The first simple case is
m=2
.

Corollary 4.2
.

In the same setting as Theorem
4.1

(
for
m=2)
, we have

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}\big(x_{1},x_{2};N,q,q^{\theta}\big)=\frac{q^{-(N-1)\theta^{2}+{\theta+1\choose 2}}\cdot[\theta(N-1)-1]_{q}!}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta(N-1)-1}{\big(x_{1}-q^{j-\theta}\big)\big(x_{2}-q^{j-\theta}\big)}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{P_{\lambda}\big(x_1, x_2; N, q, q^{\theta}\big) = }{}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q,\theta}^{(2)}\left\{\frac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{2}{P_{\lambda}\big(x_{i};N,q,q^{\theta}\big)\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\big(x_{i}-q^{j-\theta}\big)}}}{[\theta N-1]_{q}!}\right\}

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q,\theta}^{(2)}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\left(\frac{1}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\circ(D_{q,x_{2}}-D_{q,x_{1}})\right)^{\theta}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q, \theta}^{(2)} }{}=\underbrace{\left(\frac{1}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\circ(D_{q,x_{2}}-D_{q,x_{1}})\right)\circ\cdots\circ\left(\frac{1}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\circ(D_{q,x_{2}}-D_{q,x_{1}})\right)}_{\textrm{composition of $\theta$ operators}}.

Proof.

For
m=2
, we have
M_{\theta}^{(2)}=\left\{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&n\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right],\ n\in\{0,1,\dots,\theta\}\right\}
,
and thus the operator
\mathcal{D}_{q,\theta}^{(2)}
becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(2)}_{q,\theta}=\frac{1}{(q-1)^{\theta}}\sum_{n=0}^{\theta}\big\{{C_{n}^{(q,q^{\theta})}\big(x_{2}^{-1}x_{1}q^{-\theta}\big)}T_{q,x_{1}}^{n}T_{q,x_{2}}^{\theta-n}\big\}.

We let

\displaystyle a_{n}^{(\theta)}=\frac{C_{n}^{(q,q^{\theta})}\big(x_{2}^{-1}x_{1}q^{-\theta}\big)}{\prod\limits_{i=0}^{\theta-1}{(x_{1}-q^{i}x_{2})}},\qquad 0\leq n\leq\theta.

The statement of the theorem can be easily reduced to prove that the coefficient of
T_{q,x_{1}}^{n}T_{q,x_{2}}^{\theta-n}
in the product

\displaystyle D_{q,\theta}=\left(\frac{1}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\circ(T_{q,x_{2}}-T_{q,x_{1}})\right)^{\theta}=\sum_{n=0}^{\theta}{b_{n}^{(\theta)}T_{q,x_{1}}^{n}T_{q,x_{2}}^{\theta-n}}

is equal to
a_{n}^{(\theta)}
, i.e., we prove
a_{n}^{(\theta)}=b_{n}^{(\theta)}
for all
\theta,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leq n\leq\theta
, and we do it by induction on
\theta
. The case
\theta=1
can be easily dealt with, using Lemma
B.2
. Now assume
a_{n}^{(\theta-1)}=b_{n}^{(\theta-1)}
for all
0\leq n\leq\theta-1
, and some
\theta\geq 2
. We prove
a_{n}^{(\theta)}=b_{n}^{(\theta)}
for all
0\leq n\leq\theta
. Evidently,
D_{q,\theta}=\big(\frac{1}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\circ(T_{q,x_{2}}-T_{q,x_{1}})\big)\circ D_{q,\theta-1}
implies

\displaystyle b_{n}^{(\theta)}=\frac{1}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\big(T_{q,x_{2}}b_{n}^{(\theta-1)}-T_{q,x_{1}}b_{n-1}^{(\theta-1)}\big),\qquad n=1,2,\dots,\theta-1,

\displaystyle b_{0}^{(\theta)}=\frac{1}{x_{1}-x_{2}}T_{q,x_{2}}b_{0}^{(\theta-1)},\qquad b_{\theta}^{(\theta)}=\frac{1}{x_{2}-x_{1}}T_{q,x_{1}}b_{\theta-1}^{(\theta-1)}.

From Lemma
B.3
in Appendix
B
, the terms
a_{n}^{(\theta)}
satisfy

\displaystyle a_{n}^{(\theta)}=\frac{1}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\big(T_{q,x_{2}}a_{n}^{(\theta-1)}-T_{q,x_{1}}a_{n-1}^{(\theta-1)}\big),\qquad n=1,2,\dots,\theta-1,

\displaystyle a_{0}^{(\theta)}=\frac{1}{\prod\limits_{i=0}^{\theta-1}{(x_{1}-q^{i}x_{2})}},\qquad a_{\theta}^{(\theta)}=\frac{1}{\prod\limits_{i=0}^{\theta-1}{(x_{2}-q^{i}x_{1})}}.

It is not difficult to conclude from these relations, and the inductive hypothesis
a_{m}^{(\theta-1)}=b_{m}^{(\theta-1)}

\forall\,m=0,1,\dots,\theta-1
, that
a_{n}^{(\theta)}=b_{n}^{(\theta)}
for all
0\leq n\leq\theta
, as desired.
∎

When
\theta=1
(equivalently
t=q
), the result has a compact form as well. Let us recall that when
t=q
, the Macdonald polynomials become the well known
Schur polynomials

s_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})=P_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};q,q)
. The Schur (Laurent) polynomials
s_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, can also be defined by the simple determinantal formula

\displaystyle s_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})=\frac{\det\big[x_{i}^{N+1-j}\big]_{i,j=1}^{N}}{\prod\limits_{1\leq i<j\leq N}{(x_{i}-x_{j})}}.

For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
1\leq m\leq N
, we consider

\displaystyle s_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};N,q)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\frac{s_{\lambda}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},1,q,\dots,q^{N-1-m}\big)}{s_{\lambda}(1,q,\dots,q^{N-1})}

and call it a
q
-Schur character
of rank
N
, number of variables
m
and parametrized by
\lambda
; it was defined before in
[
21
]
. We recover the following theorem.

Corollary 4.3

(
[
24
, Theorem 3.5]
)
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
. Then

\displaystyle s_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};N,q)=\frac{q^{{m+1\choose 3}-(N-1){m\choose 2}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}{[N-i]_{q}!}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{N-m}{\big(x_{i}-q^{j-1}\big)}}}\frac{1}{\Delta(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})}

\displaystyle\hphantom{s_{\lambda}(x_1, \ldots, x_m; N, q) =}{}\times\mathcal{D}_{q}^{(m)}\left\{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}{\frac{s_{\lambda}(x_{i};N,q)\prod\limits_{j=1}^{N-1}{\big(x_{i}-q^{j-1}\big)}}{[N-1]_{q}!}}\right\},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{q}^{(m)}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\det\big[D_{q,x_{i}}^{j-1}\big]_{i,j=1}^{m}=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}{(D_{q,x_{j}}-D_{q,x_{i}})}.

Proof.

Letting
\theta=1
in Theorem
4.1
, we see that the equation above holds if
\mathcal{D}_{q,1}^{(m)}=\mathcal{D}_{q}^{(m)}
. In fact, thanks to Lemma
B.2
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{q,1}=(q-1)^{-{m\choose 2}}\sum_{\tau\in M^{(m)}_{1}}\prod_{s=1}^{m-1}{(-1)^{\tau_{1,s+1}+\dots+\tau_{s,s+1}}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}{T_{q,x_{i}}^{i-1+\tau_{i}^{+}-\tau_{i}^{-}}}.

Evidently
\mathcal{D}_{q}^{(m)}=\prod\limits_{i<j}{(D_{q,x_{j}}-D_{q,x_{i}})}=(q-1)^{-{m\choose 2}}\prod\limits_{i<j}{(T_{q,x_{j}}-T_{q,x_{i}})}
, thus we need to show

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}{(T_{q,x_{j}}-T_{q,x_{i}})}=\sum_{\tau\in M^{(m)}_{1}}{(-1)^{|\tau|}\prod_{k=1}^{m}{T_{q,x_{k}}^{k-1+\tau_{k}^{+}-\tau_{k}^{-}}}},

(4.4)

where we denoted by
|\tau|
to the sum of all entries of
\tau\in M_{1}^{(m)}
. The operators
T_{q,x_{1}},\dots,T_{q,x_{m}}
pairwise commute. When expanding the left-hand side of (
4.4
), it is clear that the resulting terms can be parametrized by matrices in
M_{1}^{(m)}
: the term corresponding to
\tau\in M_{1}^{(m)}
is the product of
(-1)^{k}T_{q,x_{i}}^{k}T_{q,x_{j}}^{1-k}
, where
k\in\{0,1\}
ranges over the elements of
\tau
strictly above the main diagonal. Thus the term corresponding to
\tau
is

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}{(-1)^{\tau_{i,j}}T_{q,x_{i}}^{\tau_{i,j}}T_{q,x_{j}}^{1-\tau_{i,j}}}=(-1)^{\sum\limits_{1\leq i<j\leq m}{\tau_{i,j}}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}{T_{q,x_{i}}^{\tau_{i,j}}T_{q,x_{j}}^{1-\tau_{i,j}}}.

We are left to show

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}{T_{q,x_{i}}^{\tau_{i,j}}T_{q,x_{j}}^{1-\tau_{i,j}}}=\prod_{k=1}^{m}{T_{q,x_{k}}^{k-1+\tau_{k}^{+}-\tau_{k}^{-}}}.

(4.5)

Both sides of (
4.5
) are of the form
T_{q,x_{1}}^{p_{1}}\cdots T_{q,x_{m}}^{p_{m}}
, for some
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, so we simply need to check the equality between exponents
p_{k}
of
T_{q,x_{k}}
in both sides, for an arbitrary
1\leq k\leq m
. In the left side, there are
k-1
factors of the form
T_{q,x_{i}}^{\tau_{i,k}}T_{q,x_{k}}^{1-\tau_{i,k}}
,
i\leq k-1
, which overall contribute
k-1-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}{\tau_{i,k}}=k-1-\tau_{k}^{-}
to the exponent of
T_{q,x_{k}}
. Moreover there are
m-k
factors of the form
T_{q,x_{k}}^{\tau_{k,j}}T_{q,x_{j}}^{1-\tau_{k,j}}
,
k+1\leq i
, which contribute
\sum\limits_{i=k+1}^{m}{\tau_{k,i}}=\tau_{k}^{+}
to the exponent of
T_{q,x_{k}}
. Therefore the power of
T_{q,x_{k}}
in the left-hand side of (
4.5
) is
T_{q,x_{k}}^{k-1+\tau_{k}^{+}-\tau_{k}^{-}}
. Evidently, the power of
T_{q,x_{k}}
in the right-hand side of (
4.5
) if also
T_{q,x_{k}}^{k-1+\tau_{k}^{+}-\tau_{k}^{-}}
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Example 4.4
.

We discuss the first nontrivial example of the multiplicative formula for Macdonald polynomials (an example that is not dealt with in the Corollaries above):
\theta=2
,
m=3
. The formula in this case is

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}\big(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3};N,q,q^{2}\big)=\frac{q^{25-12N}}{([2N-1]_{q})^{2}([2N-2]_{q})^{2}[2N-3]_{q}[2N-4]_{q}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{P_{\lambda}\big(x_1, x_2, x_3; N, q, q^2\big) =}{}\times\frac{1}{\prod\limits_{j=-1}^{2N-7}{(x_{1}-q^{j})(x_{2}-q^{j})(x_{3}-q^{j})}}\frac{1}{\prod\limits_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}{(x_{i}-x_{j})(x_{i}-qx_{j})}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{P_{\lambda}\big(x_1, x_2, x_3; N, q, q^2\big) =}{}\times\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{q,2}^{(3)}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{3}{P_{\lambda}\big(x_{i};N,q,q^{2}\big)\prod_{j=-1}^{2N-3}{(x_{i}-q^{j})}}\right\},

the
q
-difference operator is

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{q,2}^{(3)}=\frac{1}{(q-1)^{6}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}\\
0\leq a,b,c\leq 2\end{subarray}}\big\{C^{(q,q^{2})}_{a}\big(x_{2}^{-1}x_{1}q^{-2+b-c}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{q, 2}^{(3)} = \frac{1}{(q - 1)^{6}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a, b, c\in\mathbb{Z}\\
0\leq a, b, c\leq 2\end{subarray}}}{}\times C^{(q,q^{2})}_{b,c}\big(x_{3}^{-1}x_{1}q^{-2},x_{3}^{-1}x_{2}q^{-2}\big)T_{q,x_{1}}^{a+b}T_{q,x_{2}}^{2+c-a}T_{q,x_{3}}^{4-b-c}\big\}.

We can also write the
q
-difference operator in terms of the
q
-degree operators
\{D_{q,x_{i}}\colon i=1,2,3\}
by using
\frac{1}{q-1}T_{q,x_{i}}=\frac{1}{q-1}+D_{q,x_{i}}
. Then we can replace the operator
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{q,2}^{(3)}
with the sum of operators
\mathcal{D}_{q,2}^{(3,{\rm top})}+\mathcal{A}_{q,2}^{(3)}
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{q,2}^{(3,{\rm top})}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}\\
0\leq a,b,c\leq 2\end{subarray}}\big\{C^{(q,q^{2})}_{a}\big(x_{2}^{-1}x_{1}q^{-2+b-c}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathcal{D}_{q, 2}^{(3, {\rm top})} = \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a, b, c\in\mathbb{Z}\\
0\leq a, b, c\leq 2\end{subarray}}}{}\times C^{(q,q^{2})}_{b,c}\big(x_{3}^{-1}x_{1}q^{-2},x_{3}^{-1}x_{2}q^{-2}\big)D_{q,x_{1}}^{a+b}D_{q,x_{2}}^{2+c-a}D_{q,x_{3}}^{4-b-c}\big\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{q,2}^{(3)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\geq 0\\
i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}\leq 5\end{subarray}}{\frac{f_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3};q,2)}{(q-1)^{6-i_{1}-i_{2}-i_{3}}}D_{q,x_{1}}^{i_{1}}D_{q,x_{2}}^{i_{2}}D_{q,x_{3}}^{i_{3}}},

and
f_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3};q,2)
are certain rational functions. With the help of Sage, we found

\displaystyle f_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3};q,2)=0,\qquad\textrm{for all}\quad i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}\leq 2.

There are nontrivial rational functions
f_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3};q,2)
as well, for some
i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}\geq 3
, e.g.,

\displaystyle f_{4,1,0}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3};q,2)=-(q-1)\frac{(x_{2}+x_{3})(x_{1}-qx_{3})(x_{1}-qx_{2})}{(qx_{2}-x_{3})(qx_{1}-x_{3})(qx_{1}-x_{2})},

(4.6)

\displaystyle f_{2,1,1}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3};q,2)=-(q-1)^{2}\frac{(q+1)(x_{2}-x_{3})\big(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}x_{3}+2x_{1}x_{2}+2x_{1}x_{3}\big)}{(qx_{1}-x_{2})(qx_{1}-x_{3})(qx_{2}-x_{3})}.

(4.7)

Two important observations are in order. First, from Corollaries
4.2
and
4.3
, one might believe that
\mathcal{D}_{q,\theta}^{(m)}
is, in general, homogeneous of degree
\theta{m\choose 2}
as a functions of the operators
\{D_{q,x_{i}}\colon i=1,2,3\}
. However, this example disproves it. Second, the terms
f_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3};q,2)
above make us suspect that
f_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3};q,2)
is divisible by
(q-1)^{6-i_{1}-i_{2}-i_{3}}

\forall\,0\leq i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}\leq 5
. We have checked this fact in the computer. In fact, we believe that the analogous statement for general
m
,
\theta\in\mathbb{N}
holds true, but the author could not prove it.

4.2
Proof of Theorem
4.1

Fix a positive signature
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
and let us prove equation (
4.1
); we extend the result for all signatures
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
at the end.

Let us consider
m
positive integers
n_{1}>n_{2}>\cdots>n_{m}>\theta(N+m)
. By the index-argument symmetry, Theorem
2.2
, applied to
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
and
\mu=(n_{1}\geq\cdots\geq n_{m}\geq 0\geq\cdots\geq 0)\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
, as well as the definition of the dual Macdonald polynomials
Q_{\mu}(\cdot;q,t)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(q^{n_{1}}t^{N-1},\dots,q^{n_{m}}t^{N-m},t^{N-m-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(t^{N-1},t^{N-2},\dots,1;q,t\big)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\frac{Q_{(n_{1},\dots,n_{m})}\big(q^{\lambda_{1}}t^{N-1},q^{\lambda_{2}}t^{N-2},\dots,q^{\lambda_{N}};q,t\big)}{Q_{(n_{1},\dots,n_{m})}\big(t^{N-1},t^{N-2},\dots,1;q,t\big)}.

(4.8)

Apply the Jacobi–Trudi formula for Macdonald polynomials, Theorem
2.7
, to the numerator of (
4.8
), then multiply and divide the term parametrized by
\tau
by the product

\displaystyle\prod_{s=1}^{m}{{g_{n_{s}+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{-}}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}},

so (
4.8
) equals

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in M^{(m)}}\left\{\prod_{s=1}^{m-1}{C^{(q,t)}_{\tau_{1,s+1},\dots,\tau_{s,s+1}}\Big(\Big\{u_{i}=q^{n_{i}-n_{s+1}+\sum\limits_{j=s+2}^{m}{(\tau_{i,j}-\tau_{s+1,j})}}t^{s-i}\colon 1\leq i\leq s\Big\}\Big)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.{}\qquad{}\times\prod_{s=1}^{m}{\frac{g_{n_{s}+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{-}}\big(q^{\lambda_{1}}t^{N-1},\dots,q^{\lambda_{N}};q,t\big)}{g_{n_{s}+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{-}}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}}\times\frac{\prod\limits_{s=1}^{m}{g_{n_{s}+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{-}}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}}{Q_{(n_{1},\dots,n_{m})}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}\right\}.\!\!\!\!

(4.9)

(In equation (
4.9
), we are setting
g_{n}(q,t)=0
if
n
is a nonpositive integer.) Recall that
t=q^{\theta}
,
\theta\in\mathbb{N}
. In view of Lemma
B.1
, the only terms in the sum (
4.9
) with nonzero contributions are those parametrized by
m\times m
matrices whose entries belong to the set
\{0,1,\dots,\theta\}
; define
M^{(m)}_{\theta}
to be the set of such matrices. Notice that
n_{s}+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{-}>0
for all
\tau\in M_{\theta}^{(m)}
, because of our initial assumption on the values of
n_{1},\dots,n_{m}
. By another application of the index-argument symmetry (and of the identity (
2.4
) above), we have

\displaystyle\frac{g_{n_{s}+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{+}}\big(q^{\lambda_{1}}t^{N-1},\dots,q^{\lambda_{N}};q,t\big)}{g_{n_{s}+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{-}}(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(q^{n_{s}+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{-}}t^{N-1},t^{N-2},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}{P_{\lambda}(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t)}\qquad\forall\,1\leq s\leq m.

(4.10)

Plugging (
4.2
) into (
4.9
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(q^{n_{1}+\theta(N-1)},\dots,q^{n_{m}+\theta(N-m)},t^{N-m-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(t^{N-1},t^{N-2},\dots,1;q,t\big)}

(4.11)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{\tau\in M^{(m)}_{\theta}}\left\{\prod_{s=1}^{m-1}{C^{(q,q^{\theta})}_{\tau_{1,s+1},\dots,\tau_{s,s+1}}\Big(\Big\{u_{i}=q^{n_{i}-n_{s+1}+\sum\limits_{j=s+2}^{m}{(\tau_{i,j}-\tau_{s+1,j})}+\theta(s-i)}\colon 1\leq i\leq s\Big\}\Big)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad{}\times\!\frac{\prod\limits_{s=1}^{m}{g_{n_{s}+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{-}}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}}{Q_{(n_{1},\dots,n_{m})}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}\!\times\!\prod_{s=1}^{m}\!{\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(q^{n_{s}+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{-}+\theta(N-1)},t^{N-2},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}}\right\}\!.\!

Let us make the change of variables

\displaystyle z_{s}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}q^{n_{s}+\theta(N-s)},\qquad 1\leq s\leq m,

(4.12)

and rewrite some terms from (
4.11
) in these new variables. Clearly the left-hand side of equality (
4.11
) is the Macdonald character
P_{\lambda}(z_{1},\dots,z_{m};N,q,q^{\theta})
. It is also evident that the variable
u_{i}
in the term
C_{\tau_{1,s+1},\dots,\tau_{s,s+1}}^{(q,q^{\theta})}
can be rewritten as
u_{i}=z_{s+1}^{-1}z_{i}q^{-\theta+\sum\limits_{j=s+2}^{m}{(\tau_{i,j}-\tau_{s+1,j})}}
, for
1\leq i\leq s
. Additionally we have
q^{n_{s}+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{-}+\theta(N-1)}=T_{q,z_{s}}^{\theta s-\theta+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{-}}(z_{s})
, for all
s
, which implies that the last product in (
4.11
) can be written as

\displaystyle\prod\limits_{s=1}^{m}{T_{q,x_{s}}^{\theta(s-1)+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{-}}}\left(\prod_{s=1}^{m}{P_{\lambda}\big(z_{s};N,q,q^{\theta}\big)}\right).

Next, we need to rewrite

\displaystyle\frac{\prod\limits_{s=1}^{m}{g_{n_{s}+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{-}}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}}{Q_{(n_{1},\dots,n_{m})}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}

in terms of
z_{1},\dots,z_{m}
. From Corollary
2.4
of the evaluation identity for Macdonald polynomials, we obtain

\displaystyle g_{p}\big(t^{N-1},t^{N-2},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)=\prod_{s=(1,j)\in(p)}{\frac{1-q^{a^{\prime}(s)+\theta(N-l^{\prime}(s))}}{1-q^{a(s)+1+\theta l(s)}}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\prod_{i=1}^{p}{\frac{1-q^{i-1+\theta N}}{1-q^{p-i+1}}}=\prod_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\frac{1-q^{p+j}}{1-q^{j}}}=\frac{1}{(1-q)^{\theta N-1}}\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{(1-q^{p+j})}}{[\theta N-1]_{q}!}

(4.13)

for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
,
p>\theta N
. By similar, but more complicated, computations we find

\displaystyle Q_{(p_{1},\dots,p_{m})}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\frac{1}{(1-q)^{m(\theta N-1)-\theta m(m-1)/2}}\times\prod_{s=1}^{\theta}{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}{\frac{q^{p_{j}}-q^{p_{i}+s+\theta(j-i-1)}}{1-q^{p_{i}+s+\theta(j-i-1)}}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta(N-m+1)-1}{\big(1-q^{p_{m}+j}\big)}\cdot\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta(N-m+2)-1}{\big(1-q^{p_{m-1}+j}\big)}\cdots\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\big(1-q^{p_{1}+j}\big)}}{[\theta(N-m+1)-1]_{q}![\theta(N-m+2)-1]_{q}!\cdots[\theta N-1]_{q}!},

(4.14)

for any partition
(p_{1}>p_{2}>\dots>p_{m}>0)
with
p_{i}>\theta(N-i+1)
for all
1\leq i\leq m
. From (
4.13
) and (
4.14
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\prod\limits_{s=1}^{m}{g_{n_{s}+\tau_{s}^{+}-\tau_{s}^{-}}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1\big)}}{Q_{(n_{1},\dots,n_{m})}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1;q,t\big)}=\frac{1}{(1-q)^{\theta m(m-1)/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\frac{[\theta(N-i+1)-1]_{q}!}{[\theta N-1]_{q}!}}

(4.15)

\displaystyle{}{}\times\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\big(1-q^{n_{1}+\tau_{1}^{+}-\tau_{1}^{-}+j}\big)}}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\big(1-q^{n_{1}+j}\big)}}\cdot\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\big(1-q^{n_{2}+\tau_{2}^{+}-\tau_{2}^{-}+j}\big)}}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta(N-1)-1}{\big(1-q^{n_{2}+j}\big)}}\cdots\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\big(1-q^{n_{m}+\tau_{m}^{+}-\tau_{m}^{-}+j}\big)}}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta(N-m+1)-1}{\big(1-q^{n_{m}+j}\big)}}

(4.16)

\displaystyle{}\times\prod_{s=1}^{\theta}{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}{\frac{1-q^{n_{i}+s+\theta(j-i-1)}}{q^{n_{j}}-q^{n_{i}+s+\theta(j-i-1)}}}}.

(4.17)

Observe that we used our assumption
n_{1}>n_{2}>\dots>n_{m}>\theta(N+m)
to guarantee that (
4.13
) and (
4.14
) are applicable. We have to rewrite both (
4.16
) and (
4.17
) in terms of
z_{s}
. Let us begin with (
4.16
), which is a product of
m
terms; for
1\leq r\leq m
, the
r^{\rm th}
term is

\displaystyle\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\big(1-q^{n_{r}+\tau_{r}^{+}-\tau_{r}^{-}+j}\big)}}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta(N-r+1)-1}{\big(1-q^{n_{r}+j}\big)}}=\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\big(1-q^{-\theta(N-r)}z_{r}q^{\tau_{r}^{+}-\tau_{r}^{-}+j}\big)}}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta(N-r+1)-1}{\big(1-q^{-\theta(N-r)}z_{r}q^{j}\big)}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=(-1)^{\theta(r-1)}q^{-\sum\limits_{j=\theta(N-r+1)}^{\theta N-1}{(\theta(N-r)-j)}}\cdot\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\big(z_{r}q^{\tau_{r}^{+}-\tau_{r}^{+}}-q^{\theta(N-r)-j}\big)}}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta(N-r+1)-1}{\big(z_{r}-q^{\theta(N-r)-j}\big)}}

(4.18)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=(-1)^{\theta(r-1)}q^{-\sum\limits_{j=\theta(N-r+1)}^{\theta N-1}{(\theta(N-r)-j)}}q^{-(\theta r-\theta)(\theta N-1)}\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\big(z_{r}q^{\theta r-\theta+\tau_{r}^{+}-\tau_{r}^{-}}-q^{\theta(N-1)-j}\big)}}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta(N-r+1)-1}{\big(z_{r}-q^{\theta(N-r)-j}\big)}}.

Under the change of indexing
j\mapsto\theta(N-r+1)-j
, the product in the denominator of (
4.18
) becomes

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{\theta(N-r+1)-1}{\big(z_{r}-q^{\theta(N-r)-j}\big)}=\prod_{j=1}^{\theta(N-r+1)-1}{\big(z_{r}-q^{j-\theta}\big)},

whereas the numerator of (
4.18
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\big(z_{r}q^{\theta r-\theta+\tau_{r}^{+}-\tau_{r}^{-}}-q^{\theta(N-1)-j}\big)}=T_{q,z_{r}}^{\theta r-\theta+\tau_{r}^{+}-\tau_{r}^{-}}\prod_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\big(z_{r}-q^{\theta(N-1)-j}\big)}.

Define also

\displaystyle c(N,m,\theta)=-\sum_{r=1}^{m}{\sum_{j=\theta(N-r+1)}^{\theta N-1}{(\theta(N-r)-j)}}-\sum_{r=1}^{m}{(\theta r-\theta)(\theta N-1)},

(4.19)

which is the power of
q
coming from the terms (
4.18
), for
1\leq r\leq m
. Thus (
4.16
) equals

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{\theta m(m-1)/2}q^{c(N,m,\theta)}}{\prod\limits_{r=1}^{m}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\theta(N-r+1)-1}{\big(z_{r}-q^{j-\theta}\big)}}}\cdot\prod_{r=1}^{m}{T_{q,z_{r}}^{\theta r-\theta+\tau_{r}^{+}-\tau_{r}^{-}}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\theta N-1}{\big(z_{r}-q^{\theta(N-1)-j}\big)}\right).

(4.20)

On the other hand, (
4.17
) can be expressed in terms of the variables
z_{i}
as follows:

\displaystyle\prod_{r=1}^{\theta}{\prod_{i<j}{\frac{1-q^{n_{i}+r+\theta(j-i-1)}}{q^{n_{j}}-q^{n_{i}+r+\theta(j-i-1)}}}}=\prod_{r=1}^{\theta}{\prod_{i<j}{\frac{q^{n_{i}+r+\theta(j-i-1)}-1}{q^{n_{i}+r+\theta(j-i-1)}-q^{n_{j}}}}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\prod_{r=1}^{\theta}{\prod_{i<j}{\frac{q^{n_{i}+r+\theta(N-i-1)}-q^{\theta(N-j)}}{q^{n_{i}+r+\theta(N-i-1)}-q^{n_{j}+\theta(N-j)}}}}=\prod_{r=1}^{\theta}{\prod_{i<j}{\frac{z_{i}q^{r-\theta}-q^{\theta(N-j)}}{z_{i}q^{r-\theta}-z_{j}}}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\prod_{r=1}^{\theta}{\prod_{i<j}{\frac{z_{i}-q^{\theta(N-j+1)-r}}{z_{i}-z_{j}q^{\theta-r}}}}.

(4.21)

The denominator of (
4.21
) is
\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq m\\
0\leq k<\theta\end{subarray}}{(z_{i}-q^{k}z_{j})}
, and the numerator is
\prod\limits_{r=1}^{m}{\prod\limits_{j=\theta(N-m+1)}^{\theta(N-i+1)-1}{(z_{r}-q^{j-\theta})}}
. Then (
4.17
) equals

\displaystyle\frac{\prod\limits_{r=1}^{m}{\prod\limits_{j=\theta(N-m+1)}^{\theta(N-r+1)-1}{\big(z_{r}-q^{j-\theta}\big)}}}{\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq m\\
0\leq k<\theta\end{subarray}}{(z_{i}-q^{k}z_{j})}}.

(4.22)

Therefore after the change of variables (
4.12
), and using (
4.15
), (
4.16
), (
4.17
), (
4.20
), (
4.22
) and the identities after (
4.12
), equation (
4.11
) yields the desired (
4.1
) for any
x_{s}=q^{n_{s}+\theta(N-s)}
,
1\leq s\leq m
, such that
n_{1}>\dots>n_{m}>\theta(N+m)
, provided the equality

\displaystyle c(N,m,\theta)=\theta^{2}\dbinom{m+1}{3}-\left\{N\theta^{2}-\dbinom{\theta+1}{2}\right\}\dbinom{m}{2}

holds, where
c(N,m,\theta)
is defined in (
4.19
). This is an easy exercise.

We have just proved that the statement of Theorem
4.1
holds for all
x_{s}=q^{n_{s}+\theta(N-s)}
, for all
n_{1}>n_{2}>\dots>n_{m}>\theta(N+m)
. Since both sides of the equality (
4.1
) are evidently rational functions in
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
, an easy algebro-geometric argument shows the equality holds for all
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in\mathbb{C}
, as desired.

We still have to extend the theorem to all signatures. Let us prove (
4.1
) for an arbitrary
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
. If
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
, then we are done. Otherwise, choose any
p\in\mathbb{N}
large enough so that
\widetilde{\lambda}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}(\lambda_{1}+p,\lambda_{2}+p,\dots,\lambda_{N}+p)\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
. By homogeneity of Macdonald polynomials, we have

\displaystyle P_{\widetilde{\lambda}}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};N,q,q^{\theta}\big)=P_{\lambda}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};N,q,q^{\theta}\big)(x_{1}\cdots x_{m})^{p}q^{-\theta pNm}q^{\theta pm(m+1)/2}

and

\displaystyle P_{\widetilde{\lambda}}\big(x_{i};N,q,q^{\theta}\big)=P_{\lambda}\big(x_{i};N,q,q^{\theta}\big)x_{i}^{p}q^{-\theta pN}q^{\theta p}\qquad\forall\,1\leq i\leq m

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ T_{q,x_{i}}^{(i-1)\theta+\tau_{i}^{+}-\tau_{i}^{-}}P_{\widetilde{\lambda}}\big(x_{i};N,q,q^{\theta}\big)=q^{-\theta pN}q^{p(i\theta+\tau_{i}^{+}-\tau_{i}^{-})}x_{i}^{p}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Rightarrow\ T_{q, x_i}^{(i-1)\theta+ \tau_i^+ - \tau_i^-}P_{\widetilde{\lambda}}\big(x_i; N, q, q^{\theta}\big) =}{}\times T_{q,x_{i}}^{(i-1)\theta+\tau_{i}^{+}-\tau_{i}^{-}}P_{\lambda}\big(x_{i};N,q,q^{\theta}\big)\qquad\forall\,1\leq i\leq m.

We know that (
4.1
) holds for
\widetilde{\lambda}
; from the expressions above, it holds also for
\lambda
provided that (the powers of
q
match)

\displaystyle-\theta pNm+\frac{\theta pm(m+1)}{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}{(-\theta pN+p(i\theta+\tau_{i}^{+}-\tau_{i}^{-}))}.

The latter equation is easy to check, and the proof of Theorem
4.1
is therefore finished.

5
Asymptotics of Macdonald characters

In the remaining of the paper, starting here, we denote the Macdonald polynomial
P_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};

q,t)
simply by
P_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
.

In this section, assume that
\theta\in\mathbb{N}
and let
t=q^{\theta}
. We study the asymptotics of (certain normalization of) Macdonald characters of a fixed number
m
of variables, as the rank
N
tends to infinity and the signatures
\lambda(N)
stabilize in certain way that we define next.

Definition 5.1
.

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\{\nu=(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\dots)\in\mathbb{Z}^{\infty}\colon\nu_{1}\leq\nu_{2}\leq\dots\}

be the set of weakly increasing integer sequences. We say that
the sequence
\{\lambda(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
of signatures,
\lambda(N)\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, stabilizes to
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
if we have the following limits

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\lambda_{N-i+1}(N)}=\nu_{i},\qquad\forall\,i=1,2,\dots.

In other words
\{\lambda(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
stabilizes to
\nu
if there exists a sequence of integers
0<N_{1}<N_{2}<\cdots
such that
\lambda_{N-i+1}(N)=\nu_{i}
,
\forall\,N>N_{i}
,
i=1,2,\dots
.

5.1
Asymptotics of Macdonald characters of one variable

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
t=q^{\theta}
,
\theta\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\{\lambda(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
,
\lambda(N)\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, be a sequence of signatures that stabilizes to
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}}=\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t),

(5.1)

where

\displaystyle\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\frac{(q;q)_{\infty}}{(xq;q)_{\infty}}\frac{\ln{q}}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}{x^{z}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\big(q^{-z+\nu_{i}}t^{i};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{-z+\nu_{i}}t^{i-1};q\big)_{\infty}}}{\rm d}z},

(5.2)

and the contour
\mathcal{C}^{+}
is the infinite positive contour consisting of
\big[{-}M+\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\ln{q}},-M-\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\ln{q}}\big]
and the lines
\big[{-}M+\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\ln{q}},+\infty+\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\ln{q}}\big)
,
\big[{-}M-\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\ln{q}},+\infty-\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\ln{q}}\big)
for an arbitrary
M>\max\{0,-\nu_{1}\}
, see Fig.
2
. The function
\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)
is defined by the formula above in the domain

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\bigcap_{k\geq 1}{\big\{x\neq q^{-k}\big\}}\cap{\{x\neq 0\}},

and admits an analytic continuation to the domain
\{x\neq 0\}=\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. Moreover if
\nu_{1}\geq 0
, the function
\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)
can be analytically continued to
\mathbb{C}
.

The convergence (
5.1
) is uniform on compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
, and if
\nu_{1}\geq 0
, then it is uniform on compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}
.

Remark 5.3
.

The theorem is a direct application of the integral representation in Theorem
3.1
. If we use instead Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
, we could possibly extend the theorem for general
q,t\in(0,1)
. Since our main application does require
\theta\in\mathbb{N}
and
t=q^{\theta}
, we do not bother to pursue the more general case
q,t\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

Let us first prove that the limit (
5.1
) holds uniformly for
x
in compact subsets of
\mathcal{U}
. We use the integral representation for Macdonald characters of one variable, Theorem
3.1
, for the signature
\lambda(N)\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, and with
x
replaced by
xt^{N}
,
x\in\mathcal{U}
. Since
P_{\lambda(N)}
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
|\lambda(N)|
, then the left-hand side is

\displaystyle t^{|\lambda(N)|}\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}.

After simple algebraic manipulations, the right-hand side becomes

\displaystyle q^{-{\theta N\choose 2}}\frac{(q;q)_{\theta N-1}}{(xq;q)_{\theta N-1}}\frac{\ln{q}}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}{\frac{x^{z}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{N}\prod\limits_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{\big(q^{-(\lambda_{i}(N)+\theta(N-i)+j)}-q^{-z}\big)}}}{\rm d}z.

Therefore we conclude (make change of variables
i\mapsto N-i+1
in the inner product)

\displaystyle\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\frac{(q;q)_{\theta N-1}}{(xq;q)_{\theta N-1}}\frac{\ln{q}}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}{\frac{x^{z}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{N}\prod\limits_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{\big(1-q^{-z}q^{\lambda_{N-i+1}(N)+\theta(i-1)+j}\big)}}{\rm d}z},

(5.3)

where
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is a finite contour encloses all real poles of the integrand and no other poles. We have the limit

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\frac{(q;q)_{\theta N-1}}{(xq;q)_{\theta N-1}}}=\frac{(q;q)_{\infty}}{(xq;q)_{\infty}},

uniformly for
x
belonging to compact subsets of
\mathcal{U}
.

Next modify the contour
\mathcal{C}_{0}
into an infinite contour
\mathcal{C}^{+}
as described in the statement of the theorem. This is possible to do because all the poles of the integrands of (
5.3
) belong to the interior of
\mathcal{C}^{+}
, as
N
grows. Moreover the resulting integral is well-posed for large enough
N
since the integrand is of order
\big(|x|q^{\theta N}\big)^{\Re z}
and for any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{C}
, there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sup\limits_{N>N_{0}}\sup\limits_{x\in K}{\big\{|x|q^{\theta N}\big\}}<1
.

We now look at the asymptotics of the integral in (
5.3
), with
\mathcal{C}_{0}
replaced by
\mathcal{C}^{+}
. The denominator in the integrand has the following limit

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\prod_{i=1}^{N}\prod_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{\big(1-q^{-z+\lambda_{N-i+1}(N)+\theta(i-1)+j}\big)}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\prod_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{\big(1-q^{-z+\nu_{i}+\theta(i-1)+j}\big)}=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\big(q^{-z+\nu_{i}}t^{i-1};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{-z+\nu_{i}}t^{i};q\big)_{\infty}}}.

The limit above can be justified properly by using the dominated convergence theorem and the estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{\big|q^{-z+\lambda_{N-i+1}(N)+\theta(i-1)+j}\big|}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{q^{-\Re z+\lambda_{N}(N)+\theta(i-1)+j}}\leq c\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{q^{\theta(i-1)+j}}<\frac{c}{1-q},

valid for some constant
c>0
such that
q^{-\Re z+\lambda_{N}(N)}<c
for all
N\geq 1
(it exists because
\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\lambda_{N}(N)}=\nu_{1}
).

To prove the limit in the statement of the theorem, we are left to show

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\int_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}\frac{x^{z}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{N}\prod\limits_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{\big(1-q^{-z}q^{\lambda_{N-i+1}(N)+\theta(i-1)+j}\big)}}{\rm d}z}=\int_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}{x^{z}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\big(q^{-z+\nu_{i}}t^{i};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{-z+\nu_{i}}t^{i-1};q\big)_{\infty}}}{\rm d}z}.

We already proved the pointwise convergence; to make use of the dominated convergence theorem, we simply need estimates on the contribution of the tails of
\mathcal{C}^{+}
that are uniform in
N
and uniform for
x
belonging to compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. Parametrize the tails of
\mathcal{C}^{+}
as
z=r+\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\ln{q}}
or
z=r-\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\ln{q}}
; for
r
ranging from some large
R>1
to
+\infty
, we want to show that the contribution of each of these lines is small. We have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{x^{z}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{N}\prod\limits_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{\big(1-q^{-z}q^{\lambda_{N-i+1}(N)+\theta(i-1)+j}\big)}}\right|=\frac{\big|x^{r\pm\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\ln{q}}}\big|}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{N}\prod\limits_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{\big(1+q^{-r}q^{\lambda_{N-i+1}(N)+\theta(i-1)+j}\big)}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leq C_{1}\times\frac{|x|^{r}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\prod\limits_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{\big(1+q^{-r}q^{\lambda_{N-i+1}(N)+\theta(i-1)+j}\big)}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leq C_{2}\times\frac{|x|^{r}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\prod\limits_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{(1+q^{-r})}}\leq C_{2}\times\frac{|x|^{r}}{(q^{-r})^{\theta k}}=C_{2}\times\big(|x|t^{k}\big)^{r},

for all
1\leq k\leq N
, where the constant in the second line is uniform for
x
over compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
, and the constant in the third line depends on
k
, but not on
N
(note that to go from the second to the third line, we needed to use that
\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\lambda_{N-i+1}(N)}
exists for
i=1,\dots,k
and so the sequence
\{\lambda_{N-i+1}(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
is uniformly bounded for
i=1,\dots,k
). Now for any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
and any
M>0
, there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sup\limits_{x\in K}{|x|}\cdot t^{k}\leq e^{-M}
. Since
\int_{R}^{\infty}{e^{-Mr}{\rm d}r}=e^{-MR}/M\xrightarrow{R\rightarrow\infty}0
, we have shown that the contribution of the tails of
\mathcal{C}^{+}
is uniformly small over
x
in compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
and for large enough
N
.

We have proved so far that the limit in the theorem holds uniformly for
x
in compact subsets of
\mathcal{U}
. Since the set
\{q^{-1},q^{-2},\dots\}
is discrete and has no accumulation points, Cauchy’s integral formula allows us to deduce the uniform convergence in compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
as soon as we show that
\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)
admits an analytic continuation to
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

By virtue of Riemann’s theorem of removable singularities, it will suffice to show that
\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)
is uniformly bounded in an open neighborhood of each pole
q^{-k}
. Let
R>0
,
R\notin\{q^{n}\colon n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, be arbitrary. From what we have shown so far, it is clear that

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\frac{1}{R}\leq|x|\leq R\\
x\in\mathcal{U}\end{subarray}}\big|\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)\big|\leq\sup_{N\geq 1}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\frac{1}{R}\leq|x|\leq R\\
x\in\mathcal{U}\end{subarray}}{\left|\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}\right|}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\frac{1}{R} \leq|x| \leq R \\
x\in\mathcal{U}\end{subarray}}\big| \Phi^{\nu}(x; q, t) \big|}{}\leq\sup_{N\geq 1}\sup_{\frac{1}{R}\leq|x|\leq R}{\left|\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}\right|}.

Thanks to the branching rule for Macdonald polynomials, Theorem
2.5
, the fact that all the branching coefficients
\psi_{\mu/\nu}(q,t)
are nonnegative when
q,t\in(0,1)
, and the fact that each
P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)
is a Laurent polynomial in
x
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\frac{1}{R}\leq|x|\leq R}{\left|\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}\right|}\leq\sup_{\frac{1}{R}\leq|x|\leq R}{\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(|x|,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\sup_{\frac{1}{R} \leq|x| \leq R}{\left|\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x, t^{-1}, \ldots, t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1, t^{-1}, \ldots, t^{1-N}\big)}\right|} }{}\leq\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(R,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}\!+\!\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(R^{-1},t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}.

From the pointwise limits

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(R^{\pm 1},t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}}=\Phi^{\nu}(R^{\pm 1};q,t),

we deduce that the sequences
\big\{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(R^{\pm 1},t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)/P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)\big\}_{N\geq 1}
are uniformly bounded. As a result of the estimates above,

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\frac{1}{R}\leq|x|\leq R\\
x\in\mathcal{U}\end{subarray}}{|\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)|}<\infty.

Thus
\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)
admits an analytic continuation to all the poles in
\big\{z\in\mathbb{C}\colon\frac{1}{R}\leq|z|\leq R\big\}\cap\big\{q^{-1},q^{-2},\dots\big\}
. Since
R>0
was arbitrary (outside of a lattice), we conclude that
\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)
admits an analytic continuation to
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

If
\nu_{1}\geq 0
, then
\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\lambda_{N}(N)}=\nu_{1}
shows that
\lambda_{N}(N)\geq 0
for all
N>N_{0}
and
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
large enough. For all
N>N_{0}
, the functions
{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}/{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}
are polynomials in
x
and therefore holomorphic on
\mathbb{C}
. Similar considerations as above allow us to analytically continue
\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)
to
\mathbb{C}
in this case, and also show that the limit (
5.1
) holds uniformly for
x
belonging to compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}
.
∎

We end this subsection with a Lemma that will be used in Section
7
.

Lemma 5.4
.

If
\nu,\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N}
are such that

\displaystyle\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)=\Phi^{\widetilde{\nu}}(x;q,t)\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T},

(5.4)

then
\nu=\widetilde{\nu}
.

Proof.

In the integral representation of
\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)
, the set of poles of the integrand is

\bigcup_{r=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{s=0}^{\theta-1}{\{\nu_{r}+\theta(r-1)+s\}}

and they are all enclosed by contour
\mathcal{C}^{+}
. We can therefore expand

\displaystyle\frac{\ln{q}}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}{x^{z}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}{\frac{(q^{-z+\nu_{i}}t^{i};q)_{\infty}}{(q^{-z+\nu_{i}}t^{i-1};q)_{\infty}}}{\rm d}z},

at least formally, as the sum of residues

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\sum_{s=0}^{\theta-1}\left\{x^{\nu_{r}+\theta(r-1)+s}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\geq 1\\
i\neq r\end{subarray}}{\frac{\big(q^{-\nu_{r}+\nu_{i}-s}t^{i-r+1};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{-\nu_{r}+\nu_{i}-s}t^{i-r};q\big)_{\infty}}}\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta>j\geq 0\\
j\neq s\end{subarray}}{\frac{1}{(1-q^{j-s})}}\right\}.

(5.5)

The series above converges absolutely for all
x\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
, and uniformly for
x
in compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
; in fact, we can argue as in the special case
\theta=1
of
[
24
, Section 6.2]
. We have the bounds

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta>j\geq 0\\
j\neq s\end{subarray}}{\left|\frac{1}{(1-q^{j-s})}\right|}\leq\frac{1}{(1-q)\cdots\big(1-q^{\theta}\big)\big(q^{-1}-1\big)\cdots\big(q^{-\theta}-1\big)};

\displaystyle\prod_{i>r}{\left|\frac{\big(q^{-\nu_{r}+\nu_{i}-s}t^{i-r+1};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{-\nu_{r}+\nu_{i}-s}t^{i-r};q\big)_{\infty}}\right|}=\prod_{i>r}\prod_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{\left|\frac{1}{\big(1-q^{-\nu_{r}+\nu_{i}-s+\theta(i-r)+j}\big)}\right|}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\prod_{i>r}\prod_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{\frac{1}{\big(1-q^{-\nu_{r}+\nu_{i}-s+\theta(i-r)+j}\big)}}\qquad{}\leq\prod_{i>r}\prod_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{\frac{1}{\big(1-q^{-(\theta-1)+\theta(i-r)+j}\big)}}=\frac{1}{(q;q)_{\infty}};

\displaystyle\prod_{i<r}{\left|\frac{\big(q^{-\nu_{r}+\nu_{i}-s}t^{i-r+1};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{-\nu_{r}+\nu_{i}-s}t^{i-r};q\big)_{\infty}}\right|}=\prod_{i<r}\prod_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{\frac{q^{\nu_{r}+\theta(r-1)+s-\nu_{i}-\theta(i-1)-j}}{1-q^{\nu_{r}-\nu_{i}+\theta(r-i)-j+s}}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leq\frac{1}{(q;q)_{\infty}}\prod_{i<r}\prod_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{q^{\nu_{r}+\theta(r-1)+s-\nu_{i}-\theta(i-1)-j}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leq\frac{1}{(q;q)_{\infty}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{j=0}^{\theta-1}{q^{\nu_{r}+\theta(r-1)+s-\nu_{i}-\theta(i-1)-j}},\qquad\textrm{ for any}\quad 1\leq m<r,

\displaystyle\qquad{}=q^{m\theta(\nu_{r}+\theta(r-1)+s)}\times\frac{q^{-\theta(\nu_{1}+\dots+\nu_{m})}q^{-{\theta m\choose 2}}}{(q;q)_{\infty}}.

Choose an arbitrary
m\in\mathbb{N}
and fix it.
For
r>m
, the general term in brackets at (
5.5
) has modulus upper bounded by

\big(|x|q^{m\theta}\big)^{\nu_{r}+\theta(r-1)+s}\times c(m,\theta;q),

where
c(m,\theta;q)>0
is a constant depending on
m,\theta
, as well as
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}
, but not on
r
.
It follows that the sum (
5.5
) converges absolutely for any
x\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
with
|x|<q^{-m\theta}
.
Since
m\in\mathbb{N}
was arbitrary, it follows that (
5.5
) converges absolutely for any
x\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.
The uniform convergence also follows from the bound above.
In particular, (
5.5
) is the Fourier expansion of
\frac{(xq;q)_{\infty}}{(q;q)_{\infty}}\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)
.
A similar Fourier expansion can be given for
\frac{(xq;q)_{\infty}}{(q;q)_{\infty}}\Phi^{\widetilde{\nu}}(x;q,t)
.
After multiplying equality (
5.4
) by
(xq;q)_{\infty}/(q;q)_{\infty}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\ln{q}}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}{x^{z}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\big(q^{-z+\nu_{i}}t^{i};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{-z+\nu_{i}}t^{i-1};q\big)_{\infty}}}{\rm d}z}=\frac{\ln{q}}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}{x^{z}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\big(q^{-z+\widetilde{\nu}_{i}}t^{i};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{-z+\widetilde{\nu}_{i}}t^{i-1};q\big)_{\infty}}}{\rm d}z}.

(5.6)

We can expand both sides of (
5.6
) as above, to get an equality of the form
\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}{c_{k}(\nu)x^{k}}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}{c_{k}(\widetilde{\nu})x^{k}}
.
More precisely, (
5.5
) gives that the set
\{k\in\mathbb{Z}\colon c_{k}(\nu)\neq 0\}
of indices which appear in the expansion of the left-hand side of (
5.6
) is
\{\nu_{r}+\theta(r-1)+s\colon 1\leq r,\,0\leq s\leq\theta-1\}
. Similarly,
\{k\in\mathbb{Z}\colon c_{k}(\widetilde{\nu})\neq 0\}=\{\widetilde{\nu}_{r}+\theta(r-1)+s\colon 1\leq r,\,0\leq s\leq\theta-1\}
. The equality of these sets, and the inequalities
\nu_{1}\leq\nu_{2}\leq\cdots
,
\widetilde{\nu}_{1}\leq\widetilde{\nu}_{2}\leq\cdots
, imply that
\nu_{r}=\widetilde{\nu}_{r}
for all
r\geq 1
, i.e.,
\nu=\widetilde{\nu}
.
∎

5.2
Asymptotics of Macdonald characters of a fixed number
\boldsymbol{m}
of variables

The following theorem is the main result for asymptotics of Macdonald characters of any given rank
m\in\mathbb{N}
as
N
tends to infinity.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
\theta\in\mathbb{N}
,
t=q^{\theta}
, and
\{\lambda(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
,
\lambda(N)\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, be a sequence of signatures that stabilizes to
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
. Also let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be arbitrary. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},t^{-m},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}}=\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t),

(5.7)

where

\displaystyle\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\frac{q^{-2\theta^{2}{m\choose 3}-{\theta+1\choose 2}{m\choose 2}}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}{\big(x_{i}qt^{m-1};q\big)_{\infty}}}\frac{1}{\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq m\\
0\leq k<\theta\end{subarray}}{(q^{k}x_{j}-x_{i})}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Phi^{\nu}(x_1, \ldots, x_m; q, t) \mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}}{}\times\widetilde{D}_{q,\theta}^{(m)}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\Phi^{\nu}(x_{i};q,t)(x_{i}q;q)_{\infty}}\right\},

\displaystyle\widetilde{D}_{q,\theta}^{(m)}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\sum_{\tau\in M_{\theta}^{(m)}}{C_{\tau}^{(q,q^{\theta})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\prod_{i=1}^{m}{T_{q,x_{i}}^{(i-1)\theta+\tau_{i}^{+}-\tau_{i}^{-}}}}.

(5.8)

Above we used the notation of Section
4
:
M_{\theta}^{(m)}
is the set of all strictly upper triangular matrices with entries in
\{0,1,\dots,\theta\}
, and for
\tau\in M_{\theta}^{(m)}
, we let
\tau_{i}^{+}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\sum\limits_{j>i}{\tau_{i,j}}
,
\tau_{i}^{-}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\sum\limits_{k<i}{\tau_{k,i}}
. Also for
\tau\in M_{\theta}^{(m)}
, let
C_{\tau}^{(q,q^{\theta})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
be the rational functions defined in (
4.3
). The function
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
is defined by the formula above in the domain

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{m}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}{\bigcap_{i<j}\big\{x_{i}\neq q^{k}x_{j}\big\}}\cap\bigcap_{k\geq 1}{\bigcap_{i=1}^{m}{\big\{x_{i}\neq q^{-k}t^{1-m}\big\}}}\cap\bigcap_{i=1}^{m}{\{x_{i}\neq 0\}},

and admits an analytic continuation to the domain
\bigcap\limits_{i=1}^{m}{\{x_{i}\neq 0\}}=(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})^{m}
. Moreover if
\nu_{1}\geq 0
, the function
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
can be analytically continued to
\mathbb{C}^{m}
.

The convergence (
5.7
) is uniform on compact subsets of
(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})^{m}
and if
\nu_{1}\geq 0
, then it is uniform on compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}^{m}
.

Remark 5.6
.

For
\theta=1
, our theorem has a different form than
[
24
, Theorem 6.5]
. It is not immediately clear that the two answers are the same.

Remark 5.7
.

From their definition, it is clear that the rational functions
\big\{C_{\tau}^{(q,q^{\theta})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\colon

\tau\in M_{\theta}^{(m)}\big\}
are holomorphic in a domain of the form
\bigcap\limits_{-N_{1}\leq k\leq N_{1}}{\bigcap\limits_{i<j}\{x_{i}\neq q^{k}x_{j}\}}
, for large enough
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular, all functions
\big\{C_{\tau}^{(q,q^{\theta})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\colon\tau\in M_{\theta}^{(m)}\big\}
are holomorphic on
\mathcal{U}_{m}
.

Proof.

This result is a consequence of Theorem
5.2
and the multiplicative formula for Macdonald polynomials, Theorem
4.1
. Let us give more details. As before we prove first the uniform limit on compact subsets of
\mathcal{U}_{m}
.
Begin by applying Theorem
4.1
for the signature
\lambda(N)\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
and
t^{N-1}x_{i}
instead of
x_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,m
. Since
P_{\lambda(N)}
is a homogeneous Laurent polynomial of degree
|\lambda(N)|
, the resulting left-hand side is

\displaystyle\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},t^{-m},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}.

(5.9)

As for the right side, the factor

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq m\\
0\leq k<\theta\end{subarray}}{\big(t^{N-1}x_{i}-t^{N-1}x_{j}q^{k}\big)^{-1}}\times(q-1)^{-\theta{m\choose 2}}\times\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\frac{[\theta(N-i+1)-1]_{q}!}{[\theta N-1]_{q}!}}

equals

\displaystyle(-1)^{\theta{m\choose 2}}\frac{q^{-\theta^{2}(N-1){m\choose 2}}}{\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq m\\
0\leq k<\theta\end{subarray}}{\big(x_{i}-q^{k}x_{j}\big)}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\frac{1}{\big(t^{N-i+1};q\big)_{\theta(i-1)}}}.

(5.10)

We can also obtain easily the polynomial equality

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{k}{\big(t^{N-1}x-q^{j-\theta}\big)}=(-1)^{k}q^{{k+1\choose 2}-\theta k}\prod_{i=1}^{k}{\big(1-xq^{\theta N-i}\big)},\qquad\text{for any}\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

Therefore for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}{\big(x_{i}t^{N-1}-q^{j-\theta}\big)}=(-1)^{m(k-1)}q^{m\left({k\choose 2}-\theta(k-1)\right)}\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\big(x_{i}q^{\theta N-k+1};q\big)_{k-1}}.

(5.11)

Observe also that the rational functions are invariant under the simultaneous transformations
x_{i}\mapsto t^{N-1}x_{i}

\forall\,i=1,2,\dots,m
, that is

\displaystyle C_{\tau}^{(q,q^{\theta})}\big(t^{N-1}x_{1},\dots,t^{N-1}x_{m}\big)=C_{\tau}^{(q,q^{\theta})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}).

(5.12)

By combining (
5.9
), (
5.10
), (
5.11
) for
k=\theta(N-m+1),\theta N
, and (
5.12
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},t^{-m},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}=(-1)^{\theta{m\choose 2}}q^{-2\theta^{2}{m\choose 3}-{\theta+1\choose 2}{m\choose 2}}\times\frac{1}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}{\big(t^{N-i+1};q\big)_{\theta(i-1)}}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\times\frac{1}{\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq m\\
0\leq k<\theta\end{subarray}}{\big(x_{i}-q^{k}x_{j}\big)}}\frac{1}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}{\big(x_{i}t^{m-1}q;q\big)_{\theta(N-m+1)-1}}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\times\widetilde{D}_{q,\theta}^{(m)}\left\{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}{\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x_{i},t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}(x_{i}q;q)_{\theta N-1}}\right\}.

The following limits hold uniformly for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
belonging to compact subsets of
\mathcal{U}_{m}

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\big(t^{N-1+1};q\big)}_{\theta(i-1)}}=1,\qquad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\frac{1}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}{\big(x_{i}t^{m-1}q;q\big)_{\theta(N-m+1)-1}}}}=\frac{1}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}{\big(x_{i}t^{m-1}q;q\big)_{\infty}}}.

We have moreover the following limit holds uniformly for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
belonging to compact subsets of
(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})^{m}
, because of Theorem
5.2
,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x_{i},t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}(x_{i}q;q)_{\theta N-1}}}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\Phi^{\nu}(x_{i};q,t)(x_{i}q;q)_{\infty}}.

It is not difficult to observe that if
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}
is a domain preserved by the map of multiplication by
q
, and
\{f_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
,
f
are sequences of holomorphic functions on
U
for which
\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}{f_{n}(x)}=f(x)
uniformly on compact subsets of
U
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{T_{q,x}^{s}f_{n}(x)}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{f_{n}(q^{s}x)}=f(q^{s}x)=T_{q,x}^{s}f(x)

uniformly for
x
belonging to compact subsets of
U
. As an implication, the order of the limit as
N\rightarrow\infty
and the
q
-difference operator
\widetilde{D}_{q,\theta}^{(m)}
can be interchanged. All the considerations above immediately imply the desired uniform limit for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
belonging to compact subsets of
\mathcal{U}_{m}
.

As in the proof of Theorem
5.2
, the limit in the statement will hold also uniformly for compact subsets of
(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})^{m}
if we show that
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
admits an analytic continuation to this larger domain. The extension of Riemann’s theorem for removable singularities for several complex variables,
[
45
, Theorem 8]
, shows that
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
admits an analytic continuation to all
\left(\{z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}\colon|z|\leq R\}\right)^{m}
if we showed only that
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
is bounded on
(\{z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}\colon|z|\leq R\})^{m}\cap\mathcal{U}_{m}
. The latter can be proved by repeating the argument in the proof of Theorem
5.2
above.

Finally, if
\nu_{1}\geq 0
then
\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\lambda_{N}(N)}=\nu_{1}
implies
\lambda(N)_{N}\geq 0
for large enough
N
. It follows that
P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},t^{-m},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)/P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)
is a polynomial in
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
for large enough
N
, and therefore holomorphic on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
. The same argument as in the proof of Theorem
5.2
again shows
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
admits an analytic continuation to
\mathbb{C}^{m}
and the limit holds uniformly for compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}^{m}
.
∎

6
Preliminaries on the
\boldsymbol{(q,t)}
-Gelfand–Tsetlin graph

In this section, assume
q,t\in(0,1)
. We use the notation
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}M_{k}=M
to indicate that a sequence of probability measures
\{M_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
converges weakly to
M
.

6.1
The
\boldsymbol{(q,t)}
-Gelfand–Tsetlin graph

The
(q,t)
-Gelfand–Tsetlin graph is an undirected,
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
-graded graph with countable vertices, together with a sequence of cotransition probabilities between the levels of the graph (considered as discrete spaces).

Begin by defining the set of vertices of the graph as the set of all signatures

\displaystyle\mathbb{GT}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\bigsqcup_{N\geq 0}{\mathbb{GT}_{N}},

where, for convenience, we have also included the singleton
\mathbb{GT}_{0}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\{\varnothing\}
.

The edges are determined by the interlacing constraints: edges only join vertices associated to signatures whose lengths differ by
1
and
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
is joined to
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N+1}
if and only if
\mu\prec\lambda
, i.e.,
\lambda_{N+1}\leq\mu_{N}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\mu_{1}\leq\lambda_{1}
. We also assume
\varnothing\prec(k)
,
\forall\,(k)\in\mathbb{GT}_{1}
. We call the graph with vertices and edges just described the
Gelfand--Tsetlin graph
2
2

2

GT graph, for short.
. Next we introduce a
(q,t)
-deformation of the GT graph by considering certain cotransition probabilities.

Definition 6.1
.

Consider the numbers
\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}(\lambda,\mu)
,
\mu\prec\lambda
, defined by the expression

\displaystyle\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}(\lambda,\mu)=t^{|\mu|}\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)\frac{P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N}\big)}=\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)\frac{P_{\mu}\big(t,t^{2},\dots,t^{N}\big)}{P_{\lambda}(1,t,\dots,t^{N}\big)},

(6.1)

for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N+1}
,
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
,
\mu\prec\lambda
, and where the branching coefficients
\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)
are defined in Theorem
2.5
. For convenience, also let
\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}(\lambda,\mu)=0
if
\mu\not\prec\lambda
, i.e., if
\mu
is not adjacent to
\lambda
.

The
(q,t)
-Gelfand--Tsetlin graph
3
3

3

(q,t)
-GT graph, for short.
is the sequence
\big\{\mathbb{GT}_{N},\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}\colon N=0,1,2,\dots\big\}
of data consisting of the GT graph and the
\mathbb{GT}_{N+1}\times\mathbb{GT}_{N}
matrices
\big[\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}(\lambda,\mu)\big]
,
N\geq 0
, defined above.

In general, the numbers
\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}(\lambda,\mu)
depend on the values
q
,
t
, but for simplicity we omit that dependence from the notation. By virtue of the evaluation identity, Theorem
2.3
, and the assumption
q,t\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}(\lambda,\mu)\geq 0,\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N+1},\quad\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}.

(6.2)

Moreover, the branching rule for Macdonald polynomials, Theorem
2.5
, shows

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,t^{2},\dots,t^{N}\big)=\sum_{\mu\colon\mu\prec\lambda}{\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)P_{\mu}\big(t,t^{2},\dots,t^{N}\big)}

and then

\displaystyle 1=\sum_{\mu\colon\mu\prec\lambda}{\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}(\lambda,\mu)}.

(6.3)

Equations (
6.2
) and (
6.3
) show that
\big[\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}(\lambda,\mu)\big]
is a stochastic matrix of format
\mathbb{GT}_{N+1}\times\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, for each
N\geq 0
. Thus
\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}
determines a Markov kernel
\mathbb{GT}_{N+1}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{GT}_{N}
. For this reason, we call
\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}(\lambda,\mu)
the
cotransition probabilities
. Let us also define the more general Markov kernels
\Lambda^{M}_{N}\colon\mathbb{GT}_{M}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{GT}_{N}
,
M>N
, by

\displaystyle\Lambda^{M}_{N}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\Lambda^{M}_{M-1}\Lambda^{M-1}_{M-2}\cdots\Lambda^{N+1}_{N},

or more explicitly

\displaystyle\Lambda^{M}_{N}(\lambda,\mu)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\sum_{\lambda\succ\lambda^{(M-1)}\succ\cdots\succ\lambda^{(N+1)}\succ\mu}{\Lambda^{M}_{M-1}\big(\lambda,\lambda^{(M-1)}\big)\cdots\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}\big(\lambda^{(N+1)},\mu\big)}.

By duality, the kernel
\Lambda^{M}_{N}
also determines a map
\mathcal{M}_{\rm prob}(\mathbb{GT}_{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\rm prob}(\mathbb{GT}_{N})
between the spaces of probability measures on
\mathbb{GT}_{M}
and
\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, that we denote by the same symbol
\Lambda^{M}_{N}
. For example, if
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{M}
and
\delta_{\lambda}
is the delta mass at
\lambda
, then
\Lambda^{M}_{N}\delta_{\lambda}
is the probability measure on
\mathbb{GT}_{N}
given by

\displaystyle\Lambda^{M}_{N}\delta_{\lambda}(\mu)=\Lambda^{M}_{N}(\lambda,\mu).

(6.4)

Definition 6.2
.

A sequence
\{M_{N}\}_{N\geq 0}
, such that each
M_{N}
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, is called a
(q,t)
-coherent sequence
if the following relations are satisfied

\displaystyle M_{N}(\mu)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N+1}}{M_{N+1}(\lambda)\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}(\lambda,\mu)},\qquad\forall\,N\geq 0,\quad\forall\,\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}.

(6.5)

Similarly, a finite sequence
\{M_{N}\}_{N=0,1,\dots,k}
is said to be a
(q,t)
-coherent sequence
if the relations (
6.5
) hold for
N=0,1,\dots,k-1
and all
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
.

It is clear that for any probability measure
M_{N}
on
\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, there exist probability measures
M_{0},M_{1},\dots,M_{N-1}
on
\mathbb{GT}_{0},\mathbb{GT}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{GT}_{N-1}
such that
\{M_{m}\}_{m=0,1,\dots,N}
is a
(q,t)
-coherent sequence, and moreover
M_{0},\dots,M_{N-1}
are uniquely determined by this condition: in fact,
M_{m}=\Lambda^{N}_{m}M_{N}

\forall\,0\leq m\leq N-1
.

The set of (infinite)
(q,t)
-coherent sequences
\{M_{N}\}_{N\geq 0}
is a convex set. Theorem
1.3
is, in different terms, a characterization of the extreme points of the set of
(q,t)
-coherent sequences.

6.2
The path-space
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
and
\boldsymbol{(q,t)}
-central measures

The set of
(q,t)
-coherent sequences defined before is in bijection with a class of probability measures in the path-space of the GT graph
\mathcal{T}
that we define next.

Definition 6.3
.

The
path-space
\mathcal{T}
is the set of (infinite) paths in the GT graph that begin at
\varnothing\in\mathbb{GT}_{0}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\big\{\tau=\big(\varnothing=\tau^{(0)}\prec\tau^{(1)}\prec\tau^{(2)}\prec\cdots\big)\colon\tau^{(n)}\in\mathbb{GT}_{n}\ \forall n\geq 0\big\}.

For any finite path
\phi=(\phi^{(0)}\prec\phi^{(1)}\prec\dots\prec\phi^{(n)})
, define the
cylinder set
S_{\phi}
(or simply
S(\phi)
) by

\displaystyle S_{\phi}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\big\{\tau\in\mathcal{T}\colon\tau^{(i)}=\phi^{(i)}\ \forall\,i=0,1,\dots,n\big\}\subset\mathcal{T}.

The set
\mathcal{T}
is equipped with the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the cylinder sets
S_{\phi}
, where
\phi
varies over all finite paths in the GT graph. We always consider
\mathcal{T}
as a measurable space.

An interesting class of probability measures on
\mathcal{T}
consists of the ones that are coherent with the sequence of stochastic matrices
\big\{\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}\big\}_{N\geq 0}
. To clarify what such coherence is, define the natural projection maps

\displaystyle\operatorname{Proj}_{N}\colon\ \mathcal{T}\subset\prod_{n\geq 0}{\mathbb{GT}_{n}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{GT}_{N},

\displaystyle\hphantom{\operatorname{Proj}_N\colon}{}\ \tau=\big(\tau^{(0)}\prec\tau^{(1)}\prec\tau^{(2)}\prec\cdots\big)\mapsto\tau^{(N)}.

It is a standard exercise to show that the
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathcal{T}
is the smallest one for which all the maps
\operatorname{Proj}_{N}
are measurable. Consequently, for any probability measure
M
on
\mathcal{T}
, we can associate to it the sequence of its pushforwards under the maps
\operatorname{Proj}_{N}
, namely the sequence
\{M_{N}\}_{N\geq 0}
,
M_{N}=(\operatorname{Proj}_{N})_{*}M
.

Definition 6.4
.

A probability measure
M
on
\mathcal{T}
is said to be a
(q,t)
-central measure
if the following relations hold

\displaystyle M\big(S\big(\phi^{(0)}\prec\phi^{(1)}\dots\prec\phi^{(N-1)}\prec\phi^{(N)}\big)\big)=\Lambda^{N}_{N-1}\big(\phi^{(N)},\phi^{(N-1)}\big)\cdots\Lambda^{1}_{0}\big(\phi^{(1)},\phi^{(0)}\big)M_{N}\big(\phi^{(N)}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=t^{|\phi^{(N-1)}|+\cdots+|\phi^{(0)}|}\frac{\psi_{\phi^{(N)}/\phi^{(N-1)}}(q,t)\cdots\psi_{\phi^{(1)}/\phi^{(0)}}(q,t)}{P_{\phi^{(N)}}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}M_{N}\big(\phi^{(N)}\big),

for all
N\geq 0
, all finite paths
\phi=\big(\phi^{(0)}\prec\dots\prec\phi^{(N)}\big)
, and for some probability measures
M_{N}
on
\mathbb{GT}_{N}
.
The branching coefficients
\psi_{\mu/\nu}(q,t)
are explicit in the statement of Theorem
2.5
.

One can verify easily that, if the relations above hold, then the measure
M_{N}
is the pushforward
(\operatorname{Proj}_{N})_{*}M
, for all
N\geq 0
. Moreover,
\{M_{N}\}_{N\geq 0}
is automatically a
(q,t)
-coherent sequence.

We denote by
M_{\rm prob}(\mathcal{T})
the set of
(q,t)
-central (probability) measures. The set of
(q,t)
-central measures is a convex set. The set of extreme points of
M_{\rm prob}(\mathcal{T})
, equipped with its inherited topology, is called the
boundary of the
(q,t)
-GT graph
4
4

4

We may also call it the
minimal boundary
of the
(q,t)
-GT graph, to differentiate it from the
Martin boundary
of the
(q,t)
-GT graph, see Section
7.1
below.
and is denoted by
\Omega_{q,t}
.

The following proposition implies that the correspondence between the set of
(q,t)
-central measures and the set of
(q,t)
-coherent sequences is a bijection.

Proposition 6.5
.

Any probability measure
M
on
\mathcal{T}
has an associated sequence
\{M_{N}\}_{N\geq 0}
of probability measures on
\{\mathbb{GT}_{N}\}_{N\geq 0}
, as shown above. The map
M\mapsto\{M_{N}\}_{N\geq 0}
is a bijection between the set
M_{\rm prob}(\mathcal{T})
of
(q,t)
-central measures on
\mathcal{T}
, and the set of
(q,t)
-coherent sequences. The bijection is an isomorphism of convex sets.

Let
\big\{M^{(i)}\big\}_{i\geq 1},M
be elements on
M_{\rm prob}(\mathcal{T})
and
\big\{M^{(i)}_{m}\big\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\geq 1,m\geq 0\end{subarray}}
,
\{M_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
be their corresponding
(q,t)
-coherent sequences. Then the weak limit
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{M^{(i)}}=M
holds if and only if the weak limits
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{M^{(i)}_{m}}=M_{m}
hold for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Similar statements are known for other branching graphs, e.g., the case
t=q
of our proposition is given in
[
21
, Propositions 4.4 and 4.9]
, and the case
t=q\rightarrow 1
is in
[
40
, Proposition 10.3]
.
In the case
t=q
, this proposition is given in
[
21
, Propositions 4.4 and 4.9]
.
The proof at our level of generality can be easily adapted from the proofs in
[
21
]
; details are left to the reader.
∎

6.3
Macdonald generating functions

We introduce Macdonald generating functions, which will be very helpful in our study of
(q,t)
-coherent sequences.

Definition 6.6
.

Let
M_{N}
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, then its
Macdonald generating function
