is the formal sum

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}}{M_{N}(\lambda)\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(x_{1},x_{2}t,\dots,x_{N}t^{N-1}\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}}.

Note that
\mathcal{P}_{M_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
depends on the values
q
,
t
, but we omit such dependence for simplicity.

The sum above is absolutely convergent on the torus
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{T}^{m}
. In fact, Theorem
2.5
and the fact that all the branching coefficients
\psi_{\mu/\nu}(q,t)
are nonnegative imply
\big|P_{\lambda}\big(x_{1},x_{2}t,\dots,x_{N}t^{N-1}\big)\big|\leq P_{\lambda}\big(|x_{1}|,|x_{2}t|,\dots,\big|x_{N}t^{N-1}\big|\big)=P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)
. Thus not only is
\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
well-defined as a function on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
, but also
\sup\limits_{x_{1},\dots,x_{N}\in\mathbb{T}}{|\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})|}\leq 1
. Therefore
\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{m})
.

If
M_{N}
is supported on the set of positive signatures
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
, then each
P_{\lambda}\big(x_{1},x_{2}t,\dots,x_{N}t^{N-1}\big)
is a polynomial in
x_{1},\dots,x_{N}
and therefore the sum defining
\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}
is absolutely convergent on the closed unit disk
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{D}^{m}
. Moreover
\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{D}^{m})
if
M_{N}
is supported on
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
.

In general,
\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{m})\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{m})
. The Fourier series expansion of
\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}
can be obtained by using Corollary
2.6
. In fact,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}}{M_{N}(\lambda)\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(x_{1},x_{2}t,\dots,x_{N}t^{N-1}\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathcal{P}_{M_N}(x_1, \ldots, x_N)}{}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}}{M_{N}(\lambda)\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}}{\frac{c_{\lambda,\mu}m_{\mu}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}t^{N-1}\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathcal{P}_{M_N}(x_1, \ldots, x_N)}{}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}}{m_{\mu}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}t^{N-1}\big)\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}}{\frac{c_{\lambda,\mu}M_{N}(\lambda)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}}},

where the interchange in the order of summation follows from the absolute convergence of all the sums involved. (For the absolute convergence, the nonnegativity of all coefficients
c_{\lambda,\mu}
is needed.)
From the expansion above, we can extract the coefficient of
x_{1}^{\kappa_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{\kappa_{N}}
in the Fourier series, for any
\kappa=(\kappa_{1}\geq\dots\geq\kappa_{N})\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
. In fact, such term appears only in
m_{\kappa}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}t^{N-1}\big)
with coefficient
t^{n(\kappa)}
, where
n(\kappa)=\kappa_{2}+2\kappa_{3}+\cdots+(N-1)\kappa_{N}
. Thus the Fourier coefficient of
x_{1}^{\kappa_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{\kappa_{N}}
in the expansion of
\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
is

\displaystyle f_{\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{N}}=t^{n(\kappa)}\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}}{\frac{c_{\lambda,\kappa}M_{N}(\lambda)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}}.

(6.6)

If
M_{N}
is supported on
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
, then the sum defining
f_{\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{N}}
above is finite. Indeed the only signatures with a nonzero contribution are
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
with
|\lambda|=|\kappa|
. But then
|\kappa|=|\lambda|\geq\lambda_{1}
, and there are finitely many signatures
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
with
|\kappa|\geq\lambda_{1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{N}\geq 0
. This observation will be put to use several times.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
M_{N}
,
M_{N}^{\prime}
be probability measures on
\mathbb{GT}_{N}
that are supported on
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
. If

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})=\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}^{\prime}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\qquad\forall\,(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{T}^{N}

then
M_{N}=M_{N}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Both
\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
and
\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}^{\prime}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
belong to
L^{2}\big(\mathbb{T}^{N}\big)
.
The equality of these functions implies that their Fourier coefficients agree.
From (
6.6
), this means

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}}{\frac{c_{\mu,\kappa}M_{N}(\mu)}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}}{\frac{c_{\mu,\kappa}M_{N}^{\prime}(\mu)}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}}\qquad\forall\,\kappa\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}.

(6.7)

Observe that we have restricted the sum above to
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
, because
M_{N}
,
M_{N}^{\prime}
are supported on positive signatures.
Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be arbitrary. We show that
M_{N}(\kappa)=M_{N}^{\prime}(\kappa)
for all
\kappa\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
with
|\kappa|=n
.

Let
C
be the matrix whose rows and columns are parametrized by
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
with
|\lambda|=n
, and such that its entry
C(\kappa,\mu)
is
c_{\mu,\kappa}/P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)
. Observe that
C
is a finite and square matrix.
Also let
M
(resp.
M^{\prime}
) be the column vector whose entries are parametrized by
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
with
|\lambda|=n
and whose entry
\mu
is
M_{N}(\mu)
(resp.
M_{N}^{\prime}(\mu)
). Then (
6.7
) yields
CM=CM^{\prime}
. The matrix
C
is upper-triangular with respect to the order
\geq
on signatures because
c_{\mu,\kappa}=0
unless
\mu\geq\kappa
. Moreover the diagonal entries are
c_{\mu,\mu}/P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)=1/P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)\neq 0
. It follows that
C
has an inverse and
M=C^{-1}CM=C^{-1}CM^{\prime}=M^{\prime}
. Therefore
M_{N}(\kappa)=M_{N}^{\prime}(\kappa)

\forall\,\kappa\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
with
|\kappa|=n
. Since
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
was arbitrary and both
M_{N}
,
M_{N}^{\prime}
are supported on
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
, we conclude
M_{N}=M_{N}^{\prime}
.
∎

Proposition 6.8
.

If the sequence
\{M_{N}\}_{N\geq 0}

(
resp. finite sequence
\{M_{N}\}_{N=0,1,\dots,k})
is a
(q,t)
-coherent sequence, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})=\mathcal{P}_{M_{N+1}}(1,x_{1},\dots,x_{N}),\qquad\forall\,(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{T}^{N}

for all
N\geq 0

(
resp. for all
N=0,1,\dots,k-1)
. The converse statement holds if, for each
N\geq 0

(
resp.
N=0,1,\dots,k-1)
,
M_{N}
is supported on
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
.

Proof.

Let us prove the first part. Let
\{M_{N}\}_{N\geq 0}
be a
(q,t)
-coherent sequence. By making use of the branching rule, Theorem
2.5
, the fact that
P_{\mu}
is homogeneous of degree
|\mu|
, and making a change in the order of summation, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M_{N+1}}(1,x_{1},\dots,x_{N})=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N+1}}{M_{N+1}(\lambda)\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(1,x_{1}t,\dots,x_{N}t^{N}\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N}\big)}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N+1}}{\frac{M_{N+1}(\lambda)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N}\big)}\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}}{\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)P_{\mu}\big(x_{1}t,\dots,x_{N}t^{N}\big)}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N+1}}{\frac{M_{N+1}(\lambda)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N}\big)}\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}}{t^{|\mu|}\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)P_{\mu}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}t^{N-1}\big)}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}}{\frac{P_{\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}t^{N-1})}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N+1}}{t^{|\mu|}\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)\frac{P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N}\big)}M_{N+1}(\lambda)}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}}{\frac{P_{\mu}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}t^{N-1}\big)}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}M_{N}(\mu)}=\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}).

We can easily show that all sums above are absolutely convergent, so the change in the order of summations can be justified.

Next we prove the converse statement. Assume that
M_{N}
,
M_{N+1}
are probability measures on
\mathbb{GT}_{N}
,
\mathbb{GT}_{N+1}
. Assume that they are supported on
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
,
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N+1}
, respectively, and also that
\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})=\mathcal{P}_{M_{N+1}}(1,x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
. Let
M_{N}^{\prime}
be the measure on
\mathbb{GT}_{N}
defined by

\displaystyle M_{N}^{\prime}(\mu)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N+1}}{M_{N+1}(\lambda)\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}(\lambda,\mu)}\qquad\forall\,\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}.

Since
\big[\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}(\lambda,\mu)\big]
is a stochastic matrix and
M_{N+1}
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{GT}_{N+1}
, then
M_{N}^{\prime}
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{GT}_{N}
. Moreover since
M_{N+1}
is supported on
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N+1}
, it follows that
M_{N}^{\prime}
is supported on
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
. In fact, if
\mu\notin\mathbb{GT}_{N}\setminus\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
(or equivalently
\mu_{N}<0
), then for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N+1}
, either
\lambda_{N+1}<0
in which case
M_{N+1}(\lambda)=0
, or
\lambda_{N+1}\geq 0
in which case
\Lambda^{N+1}_{N}(\lambda,\mu)=0
.

We will be done if we showed
M_{N}=M_{N}^{\prime}
. From what we have proved in the first part of the argument, we have
\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}^{\prime}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})=\mathcal{P}_{M_{N+1}}(1,x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
.
It follows that
\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}}=\mathcal{P}_{M_{N}^{\prime}}
on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
.
An application of Lemma
6.7
concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be arbitrary. If
\{M^{m}\}_{m\geq 1}
,
M
are all probability measures on
\mathbb{GT}_{N}
such that the weak convergence holds
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}{M^{m}}=M
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{P}_{M^{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})}=\mathcal{P}_{M}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\qquad\forall\,(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

(6.8)

The convergence above is uniform on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
be a very small real number. Since
M
is a probability measure, there exists
c>0
large enough so that

\displaystyle M\left(\{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}\colon c\geq\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{N}\geq-c\}\right)>1-\epsilon.

(6.9)

From the weak convergence
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}{M^{m}}=M
, there exists
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
so that
m>N_{1}
implies

\displaystyle M^{m}\left(\{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}\colon c\geq\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{N}\geq-c\}\right)>1-2\epsilon.

(6.10)

Consider the set
\mathbb{GT}_{N}^{[-c,c]}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}\colon c\geq\lambda_{1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{N}\geq-c\}\subset\mathbb{GT}_{N}
. It is clear that
\mathbb{GT}_{N}^{[-c,c]}
is finite and has cardinality no greater than
(2c+1)^{N}
. Also, since
\mathbb{GT}_{N}^{[-c,,c]}
is a finite set, there exists
N_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
m>N_{2}
implies

\displaystyle\left|M(\lambda)-M^{m}(\lambda)\right|<\frac{\epsilon}{(2c+1)^{N}}\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}^{[-c,c]}.

(6.11)

We are ready to make the desired estimate. Use (
6.9
), (
6.10
), (
6.11
) and the triangle inequality to argue that for any
m>\max\{N_{1},N_{2}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{T}^{N}}{\left|\mathcal{P}_{M^{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})-\mathcal{P}_{M}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\right|}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leq\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}^{[-c,c]}}{|M^{m}(\lambda)-M(\lambda)|\sup_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{T}^{N}}{\left|\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(x_{1},x_{2}t,\dots,x_{N}t^{N-1}\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}\right|}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}+\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}\setminus\mathbb{GT}_{N}^{[-c,c]}}{\left(M^{m}(\lambda)+M(\lambda)\right)\sup_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{T}^{N}}{\left|\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(x_{1},x_{2}t,\dots,x_{N}t^{N-1}\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}\right|}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leq\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}^{[-c,c]}}{|M^{m}(\lambda)-M(\lambda)|}+\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}\setminus\mathbb{GT}_{N}^{[-c,c]}}{\left(M^{m}(\lambda)+M(\lambda)\right)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{(2c+1)^{N}}\big|\mathbb{GT}_{N}^{[-c,c]}\big|+M^{m}\big(\mathbb{GT}_{N}\setminus\mathbb{GT}_{N}^{[-c,c]}\big)+M\big(\mathbb{GT}_{N}\setminus\mathbb{GT}_{N}^{[-c,c]}\big)

(6.12)

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leq\epsilon+2\epsilon+\epsilon=4\epsilon.

∎

A partial converse to the previous proposition is Proposition
6.10
below.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
\{M^{m}\}_{m\geq 1}
,
M
, be probability measures on
\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, supported on
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
. Assume the following convergence

\displaystyle\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{P}_{M^{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})}=\mathcal{P}_{M}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})

holds uniformly on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
, then we have the weak convergence
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}{M^{m}}=M
.

Proof.

All functions
\{\mathcal{P}_{M^{m}}\}_{m\geq 1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{M}
belong to
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{m})
. Therefore the limit
\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{P}_{M^{m}}(x_{1},\dots,

x_{N})=\mathcal{P}_{M}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
implies the convergence of the Fourier coefficients. Due to (
6.6
), this implies that, for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}{\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}}{\frac{c_{\lambda,\kappa}M^{m}(\lambda)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}}}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}}{\frac{c_{\lambda,\kappa}M(\lambda)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}}.

We show that
\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}{M^{m}(\lambda)}=M(\lambda)
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
. In fact, let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be arbitrary and let us prove
\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}{M^{m}(\lambda)}=M(\lambda)
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
with
|\lambda|=n
. Consider the finite, square matrix
C
whose rows and columns are parametrized by
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
,
|\lambda|=n
, and whose entries are
C(\kappa,\lambda)=c_{\lambda,\kappa}/P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1}\big)
.
Also let
\{M^{m}\}_{m\geq 0},M
be column vectors whose entries are parametrized by
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
with
|\lambda|=n
, and whose entries, at
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
, are
\{M^{m}(\lambda)\}_{m\geq 0},M(\lambda)
. From the limit relation above, we have the entrywise limit of column vectors
\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}{CM^{m}}=CM
. The matrix
C
is upper triangular (with respect to the order on signatures given in Corollary
2.6
) and has nonzero diagonal entries, thus it has an inverse
C^{-1}
.
Each entry of the column vectors
M^{m}=\big(C^{-1}C\big)M^{m}=C^{-1}(CM^{m})
is a finite linear combination of entries of
CM^{m}
, and the same can be said about the entries of
C^{-1}CM=M
. Thus the entry-wise limit of column vectors
\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}{M^{m}}=M
follows.

By assumption,
M^{m}(\lambda)=M(\lambda)=0
for any
\lambda\notin\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{N}
, so also
\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}{M^{m}(\lambda)}=M(\lambda)
in this case. Hence the weak convergence
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}{M^{m}}=M
is proved.
∎

6.4
Automorphisms
\boldsymbol{A_{k}}

Recall the set
\mathcal{N}
of nonincreasing integer sequences, given in Definition
5.1
. Equip
\mathcal{N}
with the topology of pointwise convergence. We denote a generic element of
\mathcal{N}
by
\nu=(\nu_{1}\leq\nu_{2}\leq\cdots)
. For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can define the continuous map
A_{k}\colon\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
by
\nu\mapsto A_{k}\nu=(\nu_{1}+k\leq\nu_{2}+k\leq\cdots)
. It is clear that
A_{k}
has inverse
A_{-k}
, so each
A_{k}
is a homeomorphism.

Similar automorphisms can be constructed for
\mathbb{GT}
and
\mathcal{T}
. In detail, we can define the map
A_{k}\colon\mathbb{GT}\rightarrow\mathbb{GT}
by
\lambda\mapsto A_{k}\lambda=(\lambda_{1}+k\geq\lambda_{2}+k\geq\cdots)
,
A_{k}\varnothing=\varnothing
, whose inverse is
A_{-k}
, and moreover it restricts to automorphisms
\mathbb{GT}_{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{GT}_{N}
for each
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. It is clear that
\mu\prec\lambda
implies
A_{k}\mu\prec A_{k}\lambda
, so the automorphism
A_{k}
of
\mathbb{GT}
induces the automorphism of measurable spaces
A_{k}\colon\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
,
\tau=\big(\tau^{(0)}\prec\tau^{(1)}\prec\tau^{(2)}\prec\cdots\big)\mapsto\big(A_{k}\tau^{(0)}\prec A_{k}\tau^{(1)}\prec A_{k}\tau^{(2)}\prec\cdots\big)
.

The same notation
A_{k}
is used to define automorphisms of the spaces
\mathcal{N}
,
\mathbb{GT}
and
\mathcal{T}
, but there should be no confusion each time it is used in the future.

We have introduced the automorphisms
A_{k}
because, in Lemma
6.12
below, we will relate the extreme central probability measures associated to
\nu
and
A_{k}\nu
.
The starting point is the following simple statement, which has nothing to do with probability.

Lemma 6.11
.

Recall the functions
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
, defined in the statement of Theorem
5.5
. Let
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
be arbitrary. The following equality holds

\displaystyle\Phi^{A_{k}\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)=t^{k{m\choose 2}}(x_{1}\cdots x_{m})^{k}\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t),

\displaystyle\forall\,(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})^{m}.

(6.13)

Proof.

As both sides of the identity (
6.13
) are analytic functions on
(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})^{m}
, we only need to prove the equality for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
, where the domain
\mathcal{U}_{m}
was defined in the statement of Theorem
5.5
. We can now make use of formula (
5.8
) for
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
. Observe that the only place where
\nu
appears in the right-hand side is inside the univariate functions
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{i};q,t)
. The operator
\widetilde{D}_{q,\theta}^{(m)}
satisfies that for any Laurent polynomial
f
on variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
, the following identity holds

\widetilde{D}_{q,\theta}^{(m)}\big\{(x_{1}\cdots x_{m})^{k}f\big\}=t^{k{m\choose 2}}(x_{1}\cdots x_{m})^{k}\widetilde{D}_{q,\theta}^{(m)}\{f\}.

We deduce that the lemma will be proved for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
once we prove it for
m=1
, that is, we need
\Phi^{A_{k}\nu}(x;q,t)=x^{k}\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)

\forall\,x\in\mathcal{U}=\bigcap\limits_{k\geq 1}{\{x\neq q^{-k}\}}\cap\{x\neq 0\}
. The latter statement easily follows from the integral definition of
\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)
in Theorem
5.2
(to be precise, our desired statement follows after a change of variables
z\mapsto z+k
in the integral).
∎

Next we introduce new maps
\{A_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
on spaces of probability measures.

For a probability measure
M_{m}
on
\mathbb{GT}_{m}
, define
A_{k}M_{m}
as the pushforward of
M_{m}
under the automorphism
A_{k}
of
\mathbb{GT}_{m}
, i.e.,
A_{k}M_{m}(\mu)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}M_{m}(A_{-k}\mu)
for all
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
. Observe that if we let
\delta_{\lambda}
be the probability measure on
\mathbb{GT}_{m}
given by the delta mass at
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
, then
A_{k}\delta_{\lambda}=\delta_{A_{k}\lambda}
for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Similarly if
M
is a probability measure on
\mathcal{T}
, define
A_{k}M
as the pushforward of
M
under the automorphism
A_{k}
of
\mathcal{T}
. This can be described concretely as follows. The automorphism
A_{k}
of
\mathcal{T}
induces automorphisms
A_{k}
on the set of paths of length
m
in the GT graph, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
:

\displaystyle\phi=\big(\phi^{(0)}\prec\phi^{(1)}\prec\cdots\prec\phi^{(m)}\big)\mapsto A_{k}\phi\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\big(A_{k}\phi^{(0)}\prec A_{k}\phi^{(1)}\prec\cdots\prec A_{k}\phi^{(m)}\big).

It is therefore natural to define also an automorphism on the family of cylinder sets by
A_{k}S_{\phi}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}S_{A_{k}\phi}
.
Then
A_{k}M
is given by
A_{k}M(S_{\phi})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}M(A_{-k}S_{\phi})=M(S_{A_{-k}\phi})
for all finite paths
\phi=\big(\phi^{(0)}\prec\phi^{(1)}\prec\dots\prec\phi^{(m)}\big)
.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
M\in M_{\rm prob}(\mathcal{T})
be a
(q,t)
-central probability measure and
\{M_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
be its associated
(q,t)
-coherent sequence. Then also
A_{k}M\in M_{\rm prob}(\mathcal{T})
and its associated
(q,t)
-coherent sequence is
\{A_{k}M_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
.

Proof.

Clearly
A_{k}M\in M_{\rm prob}(\mathcal{T})
is a consequence of the definition of
A_{k}M
as a pushforward of the probability measure
M
.
The second claim can be restated as

(\operatorname{Proj}_{m})_{*}A_{k}M=A_{k}M_{m}.

This equality follows from the definitions of
A_{k}M
and
A_{k}M_{m}
as pushforwards of
M
and
M_{m}
, the fact that
(\operatorname{Proj}_{m})_{*}M=M_{m}
, and the evident commutativity of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{GT}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{GT}_{m}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Proj}_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Proj}_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k}}

∎

7
The boundary of the
\boldsymbol{(q,t)}
-Gelfand–Tsetlin graph

In this section we prove Theorem
1.3
, which characterizes the (minimal) boundary of the
(q,t)
-GT graph. Along the way, we also define and characterize the Martin boundary of the
(q,t)
-GT graph.

Assume throughout this section that
q\in(0,1)
,
\theta\in\mathbb{N}
and set
t=q^{\theta}
.
Recall the notation
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}M_{k}=M
indicates that a sequence of probability measures
\{M_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
converges weakly to
M
.

7.1
The Martin boundary: definition and preliminaries

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, let
\delta_{\lambda}
be the delta mass at
\lambda
.
As remarked in Section
6.1
, there exists a unique
(q,t)
-coherent sequence
\{M_{m}^{\lambda}\}_{m=0,1,\dots,N}
such that each
M_{m}^{\lambda}
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{GT}_{m}
and
M_{N}^{\lambda}=\delta_{\lambda}
. Such a sequence is given explicitly by
M^{\lambda}_{N}=\delta_{\lambda}
and
M^{\lambda}_{m}=\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\lambda}

\forall\,0\leq m\leq N-1
, where the probability measures
\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\lambda}
on
\mathbb{GT}_{m}
are given explicitly in (
6.4
). Moreover recall that for a
(q,t)
-central probability measure
M
on
\mathcal{T}
, we can associate a
(q,t)
-coherent sequence
\{M_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
as in Definition
6.4
.

Definition 7.1
.

The
Martin boundary
of the
(q,t)
-Gelfand–Tsetlin graph is the subset of
(q,t)
-central probability measures
M\in M_{\rm prob}(\mathcal{T})
for which there exists a sequence
\{\lambda(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
,
\lambda(N)\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, such that the following weak limits hold

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}-\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N)}}=M_{m}\qquad\forall\,m=0,1,2,\dots.

Let us denote the Martin boundary by
\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}\subset M_{\rm prob}(\mathcal{T})
and equip it with its subspace topology, namely with the topology of weak convergence.

To characterize the Martin boundary
\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
, we begin by giving necessary conditions on sequences of signatures
\{\lambda(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
which yield a weak convergence as above. In this section, we sometimes denote
M^{\lambda}_{N}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\delta_{\lambda}
the delta mass at
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, and
\{M_{m}^{\lambda}=\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\lambda}\}_{m=0,1,\dots,N}
the corresponding
(q,t)
-coherent sequence.

Lemma 7.2
.

Assume that
\{\lambda(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
,
\lambda(N)\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, is a sequence of signatures such that
\{\Lambda^{N}_{1}\delta_{\lambda(N)}\}_{N\geq 1}
converges weakly, as
N\rightarrow\infty
, to some probability measure
\mathfrak{m}
on
\mathbb{GT}_{1}=\mathbb{Z}
. Then the sequence
\{\lambda(N)_{N}\}_{N\geq 1}
is bounded.

Proof.

We use the Macdonald generating functions of Section
6.3
above. By Proposition
6.8
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\delta_{\lambda(N)}}\big(1^{N-1},z\big)=\mathcal{P}_{M^{\lambda(N)}_{1}}(z)

and then

\displaystyle\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-2},t^{N-1}z\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-2},t^{N-1}\big)}=\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(z,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}{M^{\lambda(N)}_{1}(n)z^{n}}.

(7.1)

In the sum of the right-hand side above,
n
ranges from
\lambda(N)_{N}
to
\lambda(N)_{1}
because of the branching rule for Macdonald polynomials. We multiply the equality by
z^{-\lambda(N)_{N}}
and then set
z=0
, so in the right-hand side one clearly picks up the coefficient of
z^{\lambda(N)_{N}}
, namely
M^{\lambda(N)}_{1}(\lambda(N)_{N})
. By the index stability of Macdonald polynomials,
2.2
), the left-hand side is

\displaystyle\left.z^{-\lambda(N)_{N}}\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(z,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}\right|_{z=0}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\left.\frac{\big(z\cdot t^{-1}\cdots t^{1-N}\big)^{-\lambda(N)_{N}}}{\big(1\cdot t^{-1}\cdots t^{1-N}\big)^{-\lambda(N)_{N}}}\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(z,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}\right|_{z=0}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\left.\frac{P_{(\lambda(N)_{1}-\lambda(N)_{N},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N-1}-\lambda(N)_{N},0)}\big(z,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{(\lambda(N)_{1}-\lambda(N)_{N},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N-1}-\lambda(N)_{N},0)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}\right|_{z=0}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\frac{P_{(\lambda(N)_{1}-\lambda(N)_{N},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N-1}-\lambda(N)_{N})}\big(t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{(\lambda(N)_{1}-\lambda(N)_{N},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N-1}-\lambda(N)_{N},0)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}.

Thanks to Theorem
2.3
, we can then obtain a lower bound for
M^{\lambda(N)}_{1}(\lambda(N)_{N})
as follows:

\displaystyle M^{\lambda(N)}_{1}(\lambda(N)_{N})=\frac{P_{(\lambda(N)_{1}-\lambda(N)_{N},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N-1}-\lambda(N)_{N})}\big(t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{(\lambda(N)_{1}-\lambda(N)_{N},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N-1}-\lambda(N)_{N},0)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\frac{P_{(\lambda(N)_{1}-\lambda(N)_{N},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N-1}-\lambda(N)_{N})}\big(t^{N-2},\dots,t,1\big)}{P_{(\lambda(N)_{1}-\lambda(N)_{N},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N-1}-\lambda(N)_{N},0)}\big(t^{N-1},\dots,t,1\big)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N-1}{\frac{\big(q^{\lambda(N)_{i}-\lambda(N)_{j}}t^{j-i};q\big)_{\infty}(t^{j-i+1};q)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{\lambda(N)_{i}-\lambda(N)_{j}}t^{j-i+1};q\big)_{\infty}(t^{j-i};q)_{\infty}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}{\frac{\big(q^{\lambda(N)_{i}-\lambda(N)_{j}}t^{j-i+1};q\big)_{\infty}(t^{j-i};q)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{\lambda(N)_{i}-\lambda(N)_{j}}t^{j-i};q\big)_{\infty}(t^{j-i+1};q)_{\infty}}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}{\frac{\big(q^{\lambda(N)_{i}-\lambda(N)_{N}}t^{N-i+1};q\big)_{\infty}(t^{N-i};q)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{\lambda(N)_{i}-\lambda(N)_{N}}t^{N-i};q\big)_{\infty}(t^{N-i+1};q)_{\infty}}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}{\frac{\big(1-t^{N-i}\big)\big(1-qt^{N-i}\big)\cdots\big(1-q^{\theta-1}t^{N-i}\big)}{\big(1-q^{\lambda(N)_{i}-\lambda(N)_{N}}t^{N-i}\big)\cdots\big(1-q^{\lambda(N)_{i}-\lambda(N)_{N}+\theta-1}t^{N-i}\big)}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\geq\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}{\big(1-t^{N-i}\big)\big(1-qt^{N-i}\big)\cdots\big(1-q^{\theta-1}t^{N-i}\big)}\geq\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}{\big(1-t^{N-i}\big)^{\theta}}\geq((t;t)_{\infty})^{\theta}.

On the other hand, since
(t;t)_{\infty}\in(0,1)
and
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{M^{\lambda(N)}_{1}}=\mathfrak{m}
, then there exist
N_{1},N_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
large enough such that

\displaystyle M^{\lambda(N)}_{1}\left(\{(n)\in\mathbb{GT}_{1}\colon-N_{1}\leq n\leq N_{1}\}\right)>1-((t;t)_{\infty})^{\theta}\qquad\forall\,N>N_{2}.

We conclude that
-N_{1}\leq\lambda(N)_{N}\leq N_{1}
, for all
N>N_{2}
.
Therefore the sequence
\{\lambda(N)_{N}\}_{N\geq 1}
is bounded.
∎

One actually has the following more general statement.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume that
\{\lambda(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
,
\lambda(N)\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, is a sequence of signatures such that the sequence
\Lambda^{N}_{k}\delta_{\lambda(N)}
converges weakly, as
N\rightarrow\infty
, to some probability measure
\mathfrak{m}
on
\mathbb{GT}_{k}
. Then for any
i=1,\dots,k
, the sequence
\{\lambda(N)_{N-i+1}\}_{N\geq 1}
, is bounded.

Proof.

The argument here is very similar to that of the previous proof for
k=1
. As before, by making use of Macdonald generating functions, we can derive a more general equation than (
7.1
), which is

\displaystyle\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(z_{1}t^{1-k},\dots,z_{k-1}t^{-1},z_{k},t^{-k},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{k}}{M_{k}^{\lambda(N)}(\mu)\frac{P_{\mu}\big(z_{1}t^{1-k},\dots,z_{k-1}t^{-1},z_{k}\big)}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-k}\big)}}.

(7.2)

In the sum of the right-hand side above, note that
\mu
ranges over signatures in
\mathbb{GT}_{k}
such that

\displaystyle\mu_{k}\geq\lambda(N)_{N},\quad\mu_{k-1}\geq\lambda(N)_{N-1},\quad\dots,\quad\mu_{1}\geq\lambda(N)_{N-k+1}.

(7.3)

This is a consequence of the branching rule for Macdonald polynomials.
Another relevant observation is that for any
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{k}
satisfying (
7.3
), any monomial
c_{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}}z_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots z_{k}^{m_{k}}
with
c_{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}}\neq 0
in the expansion of
P_{\mu}(z_{1}t^{1-k},\dots,z_{k-1}t^{-1},z_{k})
satisfies:
m_{k}\geq\lambda(N)_{N}
; if
m_{k}=\lambda(N)_{N}
then
m_{k-1}\geq\lambda(N)_{N-1}
; and so on until, if
m_{2}=\lambda(N)_{N-k+2},\dots,m_{k}\geq\lambda(N)_{N}
, then
m_{1}\geq\lambda(N)_{N-k+1}
.
This is a consequence of the triangularity property of the Macdonald polynomials, see Definition/Proposition
2.1
.

In equation (
7.2
) above, multiply both sides by
z_{k}^{-\lambda(N)_{N}}
and then set
z_{k}=0
.
From the branching rule for Macdonald polynomials and the fact that the branching coefficients satisfy

\psi_{\lambda(N)/(\lambda(N)_{1},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N-1})}(q,t)=1,

the resulting left-hand side is

\frac{P_{(\lambda(N)_{1},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N-1})}\big(z_{1}t^{1-k},\dots,z_{k-1}t^{-1},t^{-k},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}.

(Note that the argument
z_{k}
is no longer present in the numerator.) Similarly, from the property

\psi_{\mu/(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k-1})}(q,t)=1,\qquad\textrm{for any}\quad\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{k},

the resulting right-hand side is

\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{k}\colon\mu_{k}=\lambda(N)_{N}}{M_{k}^{\lambda(N)}(\mu)\frac{P_{(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k-1})}\big(z_{1}t^{1-k},\dots,z_{k-1}t^{-1}\big)}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-k}\big)}}.

After that, multiply both sides by
(z_{k-1}t^{-1})^{-\lambda(N)_{N-1}}
and then set
z_{k-1}=0
; this gives

\displaystyle\frac{P_{(\lambda(N)_{1},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N-2})}\big(z_{1}t^{1-k},\dots,z_{k-2}t^{-2},t^{-k},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{k}\colon\\
\mu_{k-1}=\lambda(N)_{N-1},\ \mu_{k}=\lambda(N)_{N}\end{subarray}}{M_{k}^{\lambda(N)}(\mu)\frac{P_{(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k-2})}\big(z_{1}t^{1-k},\dots,z_{k-2}t^{-2}\big)}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-k}\big)}}.

Repeat the same procedure
k
times, until we have multiplied both sides by
\big(z_{1}t^{1-k}\big)^{-\lambda(N)_{N-k+1}}
and set
z_{1}=0
. The end result is

\displaystyle M_{k}^{\lambda(N)}\left(\lambda(N)_{N-k+1},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{(\lambda(N)_{1},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N-k})}\big(t^{-k},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)P_{(\lambda(N)_{N-k+1},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N})}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-k}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}

\displaystyle=t^{(N-k)(\lambda(N)_{N-k+1}+\lambda(N)_{N-k+2}+\dots+\lambda(N)_{N})}

\displaystyle\times\frac{P_{(\lambda(N)_{1},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N-k})}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N-k-1}\big)P_{(\lambda(N)_{N-k+1},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N})}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{k-1}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t,t^{2},\dots,t^{N-1}\big)}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{N-k}\prod_{j=N-k+1}^{N}\!{\frac{\big(1-t^{j-i}\big)\big(1-qt^{j-i}\big)\cdots\big(1-q^{\theta-1}t^{j-i}\big)}{\big(1\!-\!q^{\lambda(N)_{i}-\lambda(N)_{j}}t^{j-i}\big)\big(1\!-\!q^{\lambda(N)_{i}-\lambda(N)_{j}+1}t^{j-i}\big)\cdots\big(1\!-\!q^{\lambda(N)_{i}-\lambda(N)_{j}+\theta-1}t^{j-i}\big)}}

\displaystyle\geq\prod_{i=1}^{N-k}\prod_{j=N-k+1}^{N}{\big(1-t^{j-i}\big)\big(1-qt^{j-i}\big)\cdots\big(1-q^{\theta-1}t^{j-i}\big)}\geq((t;t)_{\infty})^{\theta k},

where we used the homogeneity of Macdonald polynomials for the second equality, as well as three applications of Theorem
2.3
for the third equality. Then
M_{k}^{\lambda(N)}(\lambda(N)_{N-k+1},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N})\geq c_{k}=((t;t)_{\infty})^{\theta k}>0
.
On the other hand, since
M_{k}^{\lambda(N)}
converges weakly to
\mathfrak{m}
, there exist
N_{1},N_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
large enough such that

\displaystyle M_{k}^{\lambda(N)}(\{(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})\in\mathbb{GT}_{k}\colon-N_{1}\leq n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\leq N_{1}\})>1-c_{k}\qquad\forall\,N>N_{2}.

Therefore we conclude that
-N_{1}\leq\lambda(N)_{N-k+1},\dots,\lambda(N)_{N}\leq N_{1}
for all
N>N_{2}
.
We conclude that each sequence
\{\lambda(N)_{N-i+1}\}_{N\geq 1}
,
i=1,\dots,k
, is uniformly bounded by a constant.
∎

Next we show that
\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
is in bijection with the set
\mathcal{N}
. We do so by first constructing a map
\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
that will later be shown to be bijective.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
M
be a
(q,t)
-central probability measure on
\mathcal{T}
and
\{M_{m}\}_{m=0,1,2,\dots}
be its associated
(q,t)
-coherent system. If
M
belongs to
\Omega_{q,t}^{{\rm Martin}}
, then there exists a unique
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
such that

\displaystyle\Phi^{\nu}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m-1}t^{-1},x_{m};q,t\big)=\mathcal{P}_{M_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}),

\displaystyle\forall\,(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{T}^{m},\quad\forall\,m\geq 1.

(7.4)

The function
\Phi^{\nu}(z_{1},\dots,z_{m};q,t)
was defined in Theorem
5.2
for
m=1
, and in Theorem
5.5
for general
m
.

Proof.

Let
M\in\Omega_{q,t}^{{\rm Martin}}
. By definition, there exists a sequence
\{\lambda(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
such that
\lambda(N)\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
,
N\geq 1
, and
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}{\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N)}}=M_{m}
holds weakly, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

By Lemma
7.3
, it follows that for any
i=1,2,\dots
, the sequence
\{\lambda(N)_{N-i+1}\}_{N\geq 1}
is uniformly bounded. In particular, since
\{\lambda(N)_{N}\}_{N\geq 1}
is uniformly bounded, there exists a subsequence
\big\{N^{1}_{1}<N^{1}_{2}<N^{1}_{3}<\cdots\big\}\subset\mathbb{N}
such that
\lambda\big(N^{1}_{1}\big)_{N^{1}_{1}}=\lambda\big(N^{1}_{2}\big)_{N^{1}_{2}}=\cdots
. Analogously, using that
\{\lambda(N)_{N-1}\}_{N\geq 1}
is bounded, there exists a subsequence
\big\{N^{2}_{1}<N^{2}_{2}<N^{2}_{3}<\cdots\big\}\subset\big\{N^{1}_{1}<N^{1}_{2}<N^{1}_{3}<\cdots\big\}
such that
\lambda\big(N^{2}_{1}\big)_{N^{2}_{1}-1}=\lambda\big(N^{2}_{2}\big)_{N^{2}_{2}-1}=\cdots
. In a similar fashion, we can define subsequences
\big\{N^{k}_{1}<N^{k}_{2}<\cdots\big\}
inductively.

Now consider the subsequence
\big\{N_{1}=N^{1}_{1}<N_{2}=N^{2}_{2}<N_{3}=N_{3}^{3}<\cdots\big\}\subset\mathbb{N}
. By construction, the sequence
\{\lambda(N_{k})\}_{k\geq 1}
is such that the limits
\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lambda(N_{k})_{N_{k}-i+1}}
exist for all
i=1,2,\dots
. Consequently, there exists
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
such that
\{\lambda(N_{k})\}_{k\geq 1}
stabilizes to
\nu
in the sense of Definition
5.1
. From Theorem
5.5
, the following limit

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\frac{P_{\lambda(N_{k})}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},x_{2}t^{2-m},\dots,x_{m},t^{-m},\dots,t^{1-N_{k}}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N_{k})}\big(1,t^{-1},\dots,t^{1-N_{k}}\big)}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\Phi^{\nu}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},x_{2}t^{2-m},\dots,x_{m};q,t\big)

(7.5)

holds uniformly for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
in compact subsets of the domain
(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})^{m}
, in particular the convergence is uniform on
\mathbb{T}^{m}
.

On the other hand, since we also have the weak convergence
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\Lambda^{N_{k}}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N_{k})}}=M_{m}
, then by Proposition
6.9
:

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{P}_{\Lambda^{N_{k}}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N_{k})}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})}=\mathcal{P}_{M_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})

(7.6)

uniformly on
\mathbb{T}^{m}
. By Proposition
6.8
and the homogeneity of the Macdonald polynomials, the Macdonald generating function of
\Lambda^{N_{k}}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N_{k})}
equals

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\Lambda^{N_{k}}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N_{k})}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=\mathcal{P}_{\delta_{\lambda(N_{k})}}\big(1^{N_{k}-m},x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\frac{P_{\lambda(N_{k})}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{N_{k}-m-1},t^{N_{k}-m}x_{1},\dots,t^{N_{k}-1}x_{m}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N_{k})}\big(1,t,t^{2},\dots,t^{N_{k}-1}\big)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\frac{P_{\lambda(N_{k})}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},x_{2}t^{2-m},\dots,x_{m},t^{-m},\dots,t^{1-N_{k}}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N_{k})}\big(1,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N_{k}}\big)}.

(7.7)

By combining (
7.5
), (
7.6
) and (
7.7
), we conclude

\displaystyle\Phi^{\nu}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},x_{2}t^{2-m},\dots,x_{m};q,t\big)=\mathcal{P}_{M_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\qquad\text{for all}\quad(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{T}^{m}

and all
m\geq 1
, as desired.
The statement about the uniqueness of
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
follows from Lemma
5.4
.
∎

Because of Proposition
7.4
, for each
M\in\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
, there exists a unique
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
such that equation (
7.4
) is satisfied for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus there is a well-defined map of sets

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}\colon\ \Omega_{q,t}^{{\rm Martin}}\rightarrow\mathcal{N},

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathbf{N}\colon}{}\ M\mapsto\nu,

(7.8)

which is determined by setting
\nu=\mathbf{N}(M)
be the unique element of
\mathcal{N}
such that (
7.4
) is satisfied. We prove below that
\mathbf{N}
is bijective, but first we show the convenient fact that
\mathbf{N}
commutes with the automorphisms
A_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, of Section
6.4
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
M\in\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
A_{k}M\in\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
and
\mathbf{N}(A_{k}M)=A_{k}\mathbf{N}(M)
.

Proof.

As
M\in\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}\subset M_{\textrm{Prob}}(\mathcal{T})
, Lemma
6.12
shows
A_{k}M\in M_{\textrm{Prob}}(\mathcal{T})
and
\{A_{k}M_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
is its corresponding
(q,t)
-coherent sequence. By definition, there exists a sequence
\{\lambda(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
,
\lambda(N)\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, such that the weak convergence holds
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N)}}=M_{m}

\forall\,m\geq 0
. We claim that
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{A_{k}\lambda(N)}}=A_{k}M_{m}

\forall\,m\geq 0
, which would show
A_{k}M\in\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
indeed.

Take any
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
, then

\displaystyle\big(\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{A_{k}\lambda(N)}\big)(\mu)=\Lambda^{N}_{m}(A_{k}\lambda(N),\mu)=\Lambda^{N}_{m}(\lambda(N),A_{-k}\mu)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\big(\Lambda^N_m\delta_{A_k\lambda(N)}\big)(\mu)}{}=\big(\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N)}\big)(A_{-k}\mu)\xrightarrow{N\rightarrow\infty}M_{m}(A_{-k}\mu)=A_{k}M_{m}(\mu),

where we have used
\Lambda^{N}_{m}(A_{k}\nu,A_{k}\kappa)=\Lambda^{N}_{m}(\nu,\kappa)
, previously stated in the proof of Lemma
6.12
.

Let us move on to the second part of the lemma. Let
\mathbf{N}(M)=\nu
; to show
\mathbf{N}(A_{k}M)=A_{k}\nu
, we need

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A_{k}M_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=\Phi^{A_{k}\nu}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m-1}t^{-1},x_{m};q,t\big)

\displaystyle\forall\,(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{T}^{m},\quad\forall\,m\in\mathbb{N}.

For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{T}^{m}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A_{k}M_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{A_{k}M_{m}(\lambda)\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(x_{1},x_{2}t,\dots,x_{m}t^{m-1}\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,t^{2},\dots,t^{m-1}\big)}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathcal{P}_{A_k M_m}(x_1, \ldots, x_m)}{}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{M_{m}(A_{-k}\lambda)\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(x_{1},x_{2}t,\dots,x_{m}t^{m-1}\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,t^{2},\dots,t^{m-1}\big)}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathcal{P}_{A_k M_m}(x_1, \ldots, x_m)}{}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{M_{m}(\lambda)\frac{P_{A_{k}\lambda}\big(x_{1},x_{2}t,\dots,x_{m}t^{m-1}\big)}{P_{A_{k}\lambda}\big(1,t,t^{2},\dots,t^{m-1}\big)}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathcal{P}_{A_k M_m}(x_1, \ldots, x_m)}{}=(x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{m})^{k}\cdot\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{M_{m}(\lambda)\frac{P_{\lambda}\big(x_{1},x_{2}t,\dots,x_{m}t^{m-1}\big)}{P_{\lambda}\big(1,t,t^{2},\dots,t^{m-1}\big)}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathcal{P}_{A_k M_m}(x_1, \ldots, x_m)}{}=(x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{m})^{k}\cdot\mathcal{P}_{M_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathcal{P}_{A_k M_m}(x_1, \ldots, x_m)}{}=(x_{1}\cdots x_{m})^{k}\cdot\Phi^{\nu}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m};q,t\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathcal{P}_{A_k M_m}(x_1, \ldots, x_m)}{}=t^{k{m\choose 2}}\big(\big(x_{1}t^{1-m}\big)\cdots(x_{m})\big)^{k}\cdot\Phi^{\nu}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m};q,t\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathcal{P}_{A_k M_m}(x_1, \ldots, x_m)}{}=\Phi^{A_{k}\nu}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m};q,t\big),

where the last equality follows from Lemma
6.11
.
∎

Proposition 7.6
.

The Martin boundary
\Omega_{q,t}^{{\rm Martin}}
of the
(q,t)
-GT graph is bijective to
\mathcal{N}
under the map
\mathbf{N}
defined in (
7.8
) above.

Proof.

Step 1.
We prove
\mathbf{N}
is surjective.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
be arbitrary; we want to show it belongs to the range of
\mathbf{N}
. From Lemma
7.5
, we may assume
\nu_{1}=0
without any loss of generality.
Consider the sequence
\{\lambda(N)=(\nu_{N}\geq\nu_{N-1}\geq\dots\geq\nu_{1})\}_{N\geq 1}
of signatures that stabilizes to
\nu
. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be arbitrary. The first claim is that the limit
\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{M^{\lambda(N)}_{m}(\lambda)}
exists for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
.

By definition (
6.4
),
M^{\lambda(N)}_{m}(\lambda)=\Lambda^{N}_{m}(\lambda(N),\lambda)=0
unless
\lambda_{m}\geq\lambda(N)_{N}=\nu_{1}=0
. Thus the previous claim is clear if
\lambda_{m}<\nu_{1}=0
, in which case
\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{M^{\lambda(N)}_{m}(\lambda)}=0
. Assume now
\lambda_{m}\geq\nu_{1}=0
, i.e.,
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
.

From Theorem
5.5
, we have the uniform limit

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m-1}t^{-1},x_{m},t^{-m},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\Phi^{\nu}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m-1}t^{-1},x_{m};q,t\big)

(7.9)

on
\mathbb{T}^{m}
. Correspondingly, the Fourier coefficients of the normalized Macdonald characters converge to those of
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m-1}t^{-1},x_{m};q,t)
. Proposition refprop:coherentsequences and the expansion of Corollary
2.6
give

\displaystyle\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m-1}t^{-1},x_{m},t^{-m},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{M_{m}^{\lambda(N)}(\mu)\frac{P_{\mu}\big(x_{1},x_{2}t,\dots,x_{m}t^{m-1}\big)}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{m-1}\big)}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{\kappa\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{m_{\kappa}\big(x_{1},x_{2}t,\dots,x_{m}t^{m-1}\big)\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{\frac{c_{\mu,\kappa}M_{m}^{\lambda(N)}(\mu)}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{m-1}\big)}}}.

(7.10)

Let
\kappa\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
be arbitrary, and denote
n(\kappa)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\kappa_{2}+2\kappa_{3}+\dots+(m-1)\kappa_{m}
. Observe that
x_{1}^{\kappa_{1}}\cdots x_{m}^{\kappa}
appears only in the monomial symmetric polynomial
m_{\kappa}(x_{1},x_{2}t,\dots,x_{m}t^{m-1})
and the corresponding term is
t^{n(\kappa)}x_{1}^{\kappa_{1}}\cdots x_{m}^{\kappa_{m}}
, so (
7.10
) is essentially the Fourier expansion of the prelimit functions in (
7.9
). Then we have that, for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
, the following limit

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{t^{n(\kappa)}\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{\frac{c_{\mu,\kappa}M_{m}^{\lambda(N)}(\mu)}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{m-1}\big)}}}

(7.11)

exists and equals the Fourier coefficient of
x_{1}^{\kappa_{1}}\cdots x_{m}^{\kappa_{m}}
in the function
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1}t^{m-1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
. As mentioned before,
M^{\lambda(N)}_{m}(\mu)=0
unless
\mu_{m}\geq\nu_{1}\geq 0
. Thus we can restrict the sum in (
7.11
) to
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
.
From the same analysis as in Proposition
6.10
, we can obtain that the limit
\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{M^{\lambda(N)}_{m}(\mu)}
exists for any
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
with
|\lambda|=n
; let us denote
M_{m}(\mu)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{M_{m}^{\lambda(N)}(\mu)}
.
Immediately from the definition (and Fatou’s lemma) it follows that

\displaystyle M_{m}(\lambda)\geq 0\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{m},

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{M_{m}(\lambda)}\leq 1.

Recall that we observed
M^{\lambda(N)}_{m}(\lambda)=0
if
\lambda\notin\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
, therefore
M_{m}(\lambda)=0
if
\lambda\notin\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
.

Next consider the following function

\displaystyle F(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{M_{m}(\mu)\frac{P_{\mu}\big(x_{1},x_{2}t,\dots,x_{m}t^{m-1}\big)}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{m-1}\big)}}.

Clearly
F
defines a function on
\mathbb{T}^{m}
, as it is defined by an absolutely convergent series on the
m
-dimensional torus. Moreover its absolute value is upper bounded by
1
, therefore
F\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{m})\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{m})
.
By the same argument preceding (
6.6
), one shows, for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
, that the Fourier coefficient of
x_{1}^{\kappa_{1}}\cdots x_{m}^{\kappa_{m}}
in
F
is

\displaystyle t^{n(\kappa)}\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{\frac{c_{\mu,\kappa}M_{m}(\mu)}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{m-1}\big)}}.

(7.12)

Observe that, if
\kappa\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
is fixed, the sums in both (
7.11
) and (
7.12
) are finite because
c_{\mu,\kappa}M_{m}(\mu)=0
unless
\mu\geq\kappa
and
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
. Therefore
\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{M_{m}^{\lambda(N)}(\mu)}=M_{m}(\mu)
for all
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
implies the equality between (
7.11
) and (
7.12
). In other words, the following convergence holds

\displaystyle\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m-1}t^{-1},x_{m},t^{-m},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}\xrightarrow{\textrm{Fourier}}F(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})

(7.13)

in the sense that all Fourier coefficients of the left side of (
7.13
) converge to the corresponding Fourier coefficients of
F(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
, as
N
tends to infinity. But we already knew that the Fourier coefficients of the left side of (
7.13
) converge to the corresponding Fourier coefficients of
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
. Therefore all the Fourier coefficients of the difference
F(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})-\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
of square-integrable functions on
\mathbb{T}^{m}
must be zero. It follows that
F(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
. In particular, the equality holds for
x_{1}=\dots=x_{m}=1
, resulting in

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{M_{m}(\mu)}=F(1,\dots,1)=\Phi^{\nu}\big(t^{1-m},t^{2-m},\dots,1;q,t\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\left.\frac{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m-1}t^{-1},x_{m},t^{-m},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}{P_{\lambda(N)}\big(1,t^{-1},t^{-2},\dots,t^{1-N}\big)}\right|_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{m}=1}}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{1}=1.

Moreover
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m};q,t)=F(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
is the Macdonald generating function of
M_{m}
.

We are almost done. For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have constructed probability measures
M_{m}
as limits of
M^{\lambda(N)}_{m}
and shown that
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
is the generating function of
M_{m}
. Complete the sequence with
M_{0}=\delta_{\varnothing}
, the delta mass at
\varnothing
. We claim that
\{M_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
is a
(q,t)
-coherent sequence. In fact, since
\{M_{m}^{\lambda(N)}\}_{m=0,1,\dots,N}
is a
(q,t)
-coherent sequence, then

\displaystyle M_{m}^{\lambda(N)}(\mu)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{m+1}}{M_{m+1}^{\lambda(N)}(\lambda)\Lambda^{m+1}_{m}(\lambda,\mu)}

(7.14)

for any
0\leq m<N
,
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
.
As
N
goes to infinity, the left side of (
7.14
) tends to
M_{m}(\mu)
.
By an argument similar to that in the proof of Proposition
6.9
, one shows that the right side of (
7.14
) converges to
\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{m+1}}M_{m+1}(\lambda)\Lambda^{m+1}_{m}(\lambda,\mu)
.
Indeed, the argument simply relies on the weak convergence
M_{m+1}^{\lambda(N)}\rightarrow M_{m+1}
and the uniform (on
\lambda
) bound
|\Lambda^{m+1}_{m}(\lambda,\mu)|=\Lambda^{m+1}_{m}(\lambda,\mu)\leq 1
.
Therefore the limit of (
7.14
) as
N\rightarrow\infty
is

\displaystyle M_{m}(\mu)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{M_{m+1}(\lambda)\Lambda^{m+1}_{m}(\lambda,\mu)},

for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
. Thus
\{M_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
is a
(q,t)
-coherent sequence and has an associated probability measure
M
on
\mathcal{T}
, as given by Proposition
6.5
. By the definition of
\mathbf{N}
, we conclude
\mathbf{N}(M)=\nu
.

Step 2.
Next we show that
\mathbf{N}
is injective.

Let
M,M^{\prime}\in\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
have the same image
\nu
under the map
\mathbf{N}
. The goal is to prove
M=M^{\prime}
. From Lemma
7.5
, we may assume
\nu_{1}=0
without any loss of generality. Furthermore, we can assume that
M
is the element of
\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
such that
\mathbf{N}(M)=\nu
and that was contructed in the first step.

Let
\{M_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
and
\{M^{\prime}_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
be the
(q,t)
-coherent sequences associated to
M,M^{\prime}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M_{1}}(x)=\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)=\mathcal{P}_{M^{\prime}_{1}}(x)\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T}.

(7.15)

As it was mentioned in Section
6.3
, Macdonald generating functions are uniformly bounded on the torus, in particular,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{m}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
for any probability measure
\mathfrak{m}
on
\mathbb{GT}_{1}=\mathbb{Z}
. Write the first equality of (
7.15
) for
x=e^{i\theta}
as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}{M_{1}(n)e^{in\theta}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}{M_{1}^{\prime}(n)e^{in\theta}}.

Both sums above are expansions of a square integrable function on
\mathbb{T}
in terms of the basis
\{e^{in\theta}\}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
. Thus the (Fourier) coefficients in both sums must agree, i.e.,
M_{1}(n)=M^{\prime}_{1}(n)

\forall\,n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and so
M_{1}=M^{\prime}_{1}
.

We aim to apply a similar argument to show that
M_{m}=M^{\prime}_{m}
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
. We will be done once this is proved, as Proposition
6.5
would then show
M=M^{\prime}
. For general
m
, we make use of the fact that
M\in\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
is the probability measure constructed in step 1: we use that each
M_{m}
is supported on
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
.

As above, the definition of the map
\mathbf{N}
implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=\Phi^{\nu}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m-1}t^{-1},x_{m};q,t\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathcal{P}_{M_m}(x_1, \ldots, x_m)}{}=\mathcal{P}_{M^{\prime}_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\qquad\forall\,(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{T}^{m}.

The equality of the functions above implies the equality of corresponding Fourier coefficients.
It follows that for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
we have, see (
6.6
),

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{\frac{c_{\mu,\kappa}M_{m}(\mu)}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{m-1}\big)}}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{\frac{c_{\mu,\kappa}M_{m}^{\prime}(\mu)}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{m-1}\big)}}.

(7.16)

When
\kappa\notin\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
(or equivalently
\kappa_{m}<0
), we claim that the left side of (
7.16
) is zero. In fact, for any
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
, either
c_{\mu,\kappa}=0
when
\mu_{m}\geq 0
or
M_{m}(\mu)=0
when
\mu_{m}<0
, because
M_{m}
is supported on
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
.
Then also the right side of (
7.16
) is zero if
\kappa\notin\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
. By using also the properties of the coefficients
c_{\mu,\kappa}
stated in Corollary
2.6
, we have

\displaystyle 0=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}}{\frac{c_{\mu,\kappa}M_{m}^{\prime}(\mu)}{P_{\mu}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{m-1}\big)}}\geq\frac{c_{\kappa,\kappa}M_{m}^{\prime}(\kappa)}{P_{\kappa}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{m-1}\big)}=\frac{M_{m}^{\prime}(\kappa)}{P_{\kappa}\big(1,t,\dots,t^{m-1}\big)}\geq 0.

Then we must have
M_{m}^{\prime}(\kappa)=0
if
\kappa\notin\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
, i.e.,
M_{m}^{\prime}
is supported on
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
. Finally an application of Lemma
6.7
says that if
\mathcal{P}_{M_{m}}=\mathcal{P}_{M_{m}^{\prime}}
on
\mathbb{T}^{m}
, and
M_{m}
,
M_{m}^{\prime}
are both supported on
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
, implies
M_{m}=M^{\prime}_{m}
, thus finishing the proof.
∎

7.2
Characterization of the Martin boundary

Our next goal is to characterize the topological space
\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
completely.
Recall the map
\mathbf{N}\colon\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
, defined above in (
7.8
), by letting
\mathbf{N}(M)=\nu
be the unique element of
\mathcal{N}
such that

\displaystyle\Phi^{\nu}\big(x_{1}t^{1-m},\dots,x_{m-1}t^{-1},x_{m};q,t\big)=\mathcal{P}_{M_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\!\qquad\forall\,(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{T}^{m},\!\quad\forall\,m\geq 1.

Proposition
7.6
shows that
\mathbf{N}
is a bijection, so the inverse map
\mathbf{N}^{-1}
is well-defined.

Definition 7.7
.

For any
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
, we denote
\mathbf{N}^{-1}(\nu)
by
M^{\nu}
and the corresponding
(q,t)
-coherent sequence by
\{M^{\nu}_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
.

From step 1 of the proof of Proposition
7.6
, and Lemma
7.5
, we have that for the sequence of signatures
\{\lambda(N)=(\nu_{N}\geq\cdots\geq\nu_{1})\}_{N\geq 1}
which stabilizes to
\nu
, the following weak convergence holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}-\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N)}}=M^{\nu}_{m}\qquad\forall\,m\in\mathbb{N},

i.e.,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N)}}(\mu)=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\Lambda^{N}_{m}(\lambda(N),\mu)}=M^{\nu}_{m}(\mu)\qquad\forall\,m\in\mathbb{N},\quad\forall\,\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}.

In fact, we note the same analysis as in step 1 of the proof of Proposition
7.6
shows that the weak convergence above holds for
any
sequence of signatures
\{\lambda(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
stabilizing to
\nu
.

We need the following lemma to prove that
\mathbf{N}
is a homeomorphism.

Lemma 7.8
.

1.

For any
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
M_{m}^{\nu}
is supported on
\{\mu=(\mu_{1}\geq\dots\geq\mu_{m-1}\geq\mu_{m})\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}\colon\mu_{m}\geq\nu_{1},\,\mu_{m-1}\geq\nu_{2},\,\dots,\,\mu_{1}\geq\nu_{m}\}
.

2.

For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
c_{m}\in(0,1)
such that for any integers
n_{1}\leq n_{2}\leq\dots\leq n_{m}
and
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
with
\nu_{1}=n_{1},\,\nu_{2}=n_{2},\,\dots,\,\nu_{m}=n_{m}
, we have
M^{\nu}_{m}(n_{m}\geq\dots\geq n_{2}\geq n_{1})\geq c_{m}
.

3.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\widetilde{\nu},\nu\in\mathcal{N}
be such that
\widetilde{\nu}_{i}\geq\nu_{i}

\forall\,i>m
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{i}=\nu_{i}

\forall\,1\leq i\leq m
, then
M^{\widetilde{\nu}}_{m}(\nu_{m}\geq\dots\geq\nu_{2}\geq\nu_{1})\leq M^{\nu}_{m}(\nu_{m}\geq\cdots\geq\nu_{2}\geq\nu_{1})
.

Proof.

Let us prove (1). We use the weak convergence of probabilities
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N)}}=M^{\nu}_{m}

\forall\,m\in\mathbb{N}
, for the sequence of signatures
\{\lambda(N)=(\nu_{N}\geq\dots\geq\nu_{2}\geq\nu_{1})\}_{N\geq 1}
. Because of the definition (
6.4
) for the maps
\Lambda^{N}_{m}
, it follows that
\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N)}(\mu)=\Lambda^{N}_{m}(\lambda(N),\mu)=0
, unless
\mu_{m}\geq\lambda(N)_{N}=\nu_{1},\dots,\mu_{1}\geq\lambda(N)_{N-m+1}=\nu_{m}
. Thus also
M^{\nu}_{m}(\mu)=0
unless
\mu_{m}\geq\nu_{1},\dots,\mu_{1}\geq\nu_{m}
.

Next we show (2); to get started fix
n_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
and let us show that
M^{\nu}_{1}(n_{1})\geq((t;t)_{\infty})^{\theta}
for any
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
with
\nu_{1}=n_{1}
(so we can set
c_{1}=((t;t)_{\infty})^{\theta}
). The weak convergence mentioned above gives
M_{1}^{\nu}(n_{1})=\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\Lambda^{N}_{1}\delta_{\lambda(N)}(n_{1})}=\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\Lambda^{N}_{1}\delta_{\lambda(N)}(\lambda(N)_{N})}
, for any
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
with
\nu_{1}=n_{1}
and
\{\lambda(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
as constructed above. The calculations in the proof of Lemma
7.2
show
\Lambda^{N}_{1}\delta_{\lambda(N)}(\lambda(N)_{N})\geq((t;t)_{\infty})^{\theta}
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and thus also
M^{\nu}_{1}(n_{1})\geq c_{1}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}((t;t)_{\infty})^{\theta}>0
, for any
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
with
\nu_{1}=n_{1}
.

For a general
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

M_{m}^{\nu}(n_{m}\geq\dots\geq n_{2}\geq n_{1})=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N)}(\lambda(N)_{N-m+1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda(N)_{N})},

for
\{\lambda(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
as constructed above.
In the proof of Lemma
7.3
, we showed
\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N)}(\lambda(N)_{N-m+1}

\geq\cdots\geq\lambda(N)_{N})\geq c_{m}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}((t;t)_{\infty})^{\theta m}
holds for all
N\geq 1
. Therefore
M_{m}^{\nu}(n_{m}\geq\dots\geq n_{2}\geq n_{1})\geq c_{m}>0
, for any
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
with
\nu_{1}=n_{1}
,
\nu_{2}=n_{2}
,
\dots
,
\nu_{m}=n_{m}
.

Finally we move on to (3). To get started, we prove it for
m=1
, so let
\widetilde{\nu},\nu\in\mathcal{N}
be such that
\widetilde{\nu}_{i}\geq\nu_{i}

\forall\,i\geq 2
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{1}=\nu_{1}
. Consider the following pair of sequences of signatures
\{\widetilde{\lambda}(N)=(\widetilde{\nu}_{N}\geq\dots\geq\widetilde{\nu}_{2}\geq\widetilde{\nu}_{1})\}_{N\geq 1}
and
\{\lambda(N)=(\nu_{N}\geq\dots\geq\nu_{2}\geq\nu_{1})\}_{N\geq 1}
. Then we have the weak limits

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}-\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\widetilde{\lambda}(N)}}=M^{\widetilde{\nu}}_{m},\qquad\mathbb{P}-\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{\Lambda^{N}_{m}\delta_{\lambda(N)}}=M^{\nu}_{m}\hskip 7.22743pt\forall m\in\mathbb{N}.

(7.17)

By the calculations in Lemma
7.2
, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda^{N}_{1}\delta_{\lambda(N)}(\nu_{1})=\Lambda^{N}_{1}\delta_{\lambda(N)}(\lambda(N)_{N})

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Lambda^N_1\delta_{\lambda(N)}(\nu_1)}{}=\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}{\frac{(1-t^{N-i})(1-qt^{N-i})\cdots(1-q^{\theta-1}t^{N-i})}{(1-q^{\lambda(N)_{i}-\lambda(N)_{N}}t^{N-i})\cdots(1-q^{\lambda(N)_{i}-\lambda(N)_{N}+\theta-1}t^{N-i})}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Lambda^N_1\delta_{\lambda(N)}(\nu_1)}{}=\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}{\frac{(1-t^{N-i})(1-qt^{N-i})\cdots(1-q^{\theta-1}t^{N-i})}{(1-q^{\nu_{N-i+1}-\nu_{1}}t^{N-i})\cdots(1-q^{\nu_{N-i+1}-\nu_{1}+\theta-1}t^{N-i})}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Lambda^N_1\delta_{\lambda(N)}(\nu_1)}{}\geq\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}{\frac{(1-t^{N-i})(1-qt^{N-i})\cdots(1-q^{\theta-1}t^{N-i})}{(1-q^{\widetilde{\nu}_{N-i+1}-\widetilde{\nu}_{1}}t^{N-i})\cdots(1-q^{\widetilde{\nu}_{N-i+1}-\widetilde{\nu}_{1}+\theta-1}t^{N-i})}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Lambda^N_1\delta_{\lambda(N)}(\nu_1)}{}=\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}{\frac{(1-t^{N-i})(1-qt^{N-i})\cdots(1-q^{\theta-1}t^{N-i})}{(1-q^{\widetilde{\lambda}(N)_{i}-\widetilde{\lambda}(N)_{N}-\theta}t^{N-i+1})\cdots(1-q^{\widetilde{\lambda}(N)_{i}-\widetilde{\lambda}(N)_{N}-1}t^{N-i+1})}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Lambda^N_1\delta_{\lambda(N)}(\nu_1)}{}=\Lambda^{N}_{1}\delta_{\widetilde{\lambda}(N)}(\widetilde{\lambda}(N)_{N})=\Lambda^{N}_{1}\delta_{\widetilde{\lambda}(N)}(\nu_{1}).

Thus taking into account the limits (
7.17
) for
m=1
, we deduce
M^{\nu}_{1}(\nu_{1})\geq M^{\widetilde{\nu}}_{1}(\nu_{1})
.

The proof of the third item for a general
m\in\mathbb{N}
follows from similar calculations that are used to prove Lemma
7.3
. We leave the details to the reader.
∎

Theorem 7.9
.

The bijective map
\mathbf{N}\colon\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
is a homeomorphism.

Proof.

The first step shows that
\mathbf{N}^{-1}
is continuous and the second one shows that
\mathbf{N}
is continuous.

Step 1.
Let
\big\{M^{\nu^{(i)}}\big\}_{i\geq 1}\subset\Omega_{q,t}^{{\rm Martin}}
and
\big\{\nu^{(i)}\big\}_{i\geq 1}
be the corresponding images under the map
\mathbf{N}
. If
\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{\nu^{(i)}}=\nu\in\mathcal{N}
pointwise, then we prove the weak limit
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{M^{\nu^{(i)}}}=M^{\nu}
.

By applying some automorphism
A_{k}
with large
k
, if necessary, and invoking Lemma
7.5
, we can assume
\nu_{1}\geq 0
. Observe that
\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{\nu^{(i)}_{1}}=\nu_{1}\geq 0
implies
\nu^{(i)}_{1}\geq 0
for large enough
i
. Thus let us also assume
\nu^{(i)}_{1}\geq 0

\forall\,i\geq 1
, for simplicity.

We reduce our desired statement to simpler ones. We claim that
\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{\nu^{(i)}}=\nu
implies

\displaystyle\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}{\Phi^{\nu^{(i)}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)}=\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)\qquad\forall\,m\in\mathbb{N}

(7.18)

uniformly on
\mathbb{T}^{m}
.

First let us deduce our desired weak convergence from the limit above.
Recall that, due to Theorem
5.5
, all functions
\big\{\Phi^{\nu^{(i)}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)\big\}_{i\geq 1}
and
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
are entire functions and, in particular, they are continuous on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{m}
. So the convergence (
7.18
) implies the convergence of Fourier coefficients. Thus for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
we obtain, see (
6.6
),

\displaystyle\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}{\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}}{\frac{c_{\mu,\kappa}M^{\nu^{(i)}}_{m}(\mu)}{P_{\mu}(1,t,\dots,t^{m-1})}}}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}}{\frac{c_{\mu,\kappa}M^{\nu}_{m}(\mu)}{P_{\mu}(1,t,\dots,t^{m-1})}}.

(7.19)

Note that in both sides of (
7.19
), the sums have been restricted to
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
. In fact, since we are assuming
\nu^{(i)}_{1}
,
\nu_{1}\geq 0

\forall\,i\geq 1
, Lemma
7.8
(1) implies that all probability measures
\big\{M^{\nu^{(i)}}_{m}\big\}_{i\geq 1},M^{\nu}_{m}
are supported on
\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
.

From Proposition
6.10
, the limit (
7.19
) yields
\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{M_{m}^{\nu^{(i)}}(\mu)}=M_{m}^{\nu}(\mu)

\forall\,\mu\in\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
. But also
M^{\nu^{(i)}}_{m}(\mu)=M_{m}^{\nu}(\mu)=0
for any
i\geq 1
and any
\mu\notin\mathbb{GT}^{+}_{m}
.
Therefore
\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{M_{m}^{\nu^{(i)}}(\mu)}=M_{m}^{\nu}(\mu)
holds for all
\mu\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
, and so
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{M^{\nu^{(i)}}_{m}}=M_{m}
. Note that we proved the weak convergence for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
. By Proposition
6.5
, we conclude
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{M^{\nu^{(i)}}}=M
.

We are left with the task of proving (
7.18
).
We show the uniform convergence in a neighborhood of the torus
\mathbb{T}^{m}
.
But it suffices to prove the limit on compact subsets of
\mathcal{U}_{m}
.
From the definition of
\Phi^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
on
\mathcal{U}_{m}
, see (
5.8
), and observing that
\mathbb{T}^{m}\subset\mathcal{U}_{m}
, it follows that the result holds for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
provided it holds for
m=1
.
We prove (
7.18
) for
m=1
on an open neighborhood of
\mathbb{T}
.

Clearly a small enough open neighborhood of
\mathbb{T}
is a subset of
\mathcal{U}
.
By the definition of
\Phi^{\nu}(x;q,t)
on
\mathcal{U}
, see (
5.2
), the desired uniform convergence will hold if we verify

\displaystyle\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}{\int_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}{x^{z}\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}{\frac{(q^{-z+\nu_{j}^{(i)}}t^{j};q)_{\infty}}{(q^{-z+\nu_{j}^{(i)}}t^{j-1};q)_{\infty}}}}}=\int_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}{x^{z}\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}{\frac{(q^{-z+\nu_{j}}t^{j};q)_{\infty}}{(q^{-z+\nu_{j}}t^{j-1};q)_{\infty}}}}

(7.20)

uniformly on compact subsets of
x\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. Note that, since all
\big\{\nu^{(i)}_{1}\big\}_{i\geq 1}
,
\nu_{1}
, are nonnegative, we can take the same contour
\mathcal{C}^{+}
for both of the integrals in (
7.20
).

The pointwise convergence of integrands in (
7.20
) is clear. We still need some uniform estimates for the contribution of the tails of the left side in (
7.20
). This is similar to the proof of Theorem
5.2
.

Let
K\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
be any compact set. Parametrize the tails of
\mathcal{C}^{+}
as
z=r\pm\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\ln{q}}
; for
r
ranging from some large
R>0
to
+\infty
, we want to show that the contribution of each of these lines is small. We have

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in K}\left|x^{z}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\big(q^{-z+\nu_{j}^{(i)}}t^{j};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{-z+\nu_{j}^{(i)}}t^{j-1};q\big)_{\infty}}}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leq{\rm const}\times|x|^{r}\times\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{\big(1+q^{-r+\nu_{j}^{(i)}+\theta(j-1)}\big)\cdots\big(1+q^{-r+\nu_{j}^{(i)}+\theta j-1}\big)}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\leq{\rm const}\cdot\frac{|x|^{r}}{q^{-kr}q^{\nu^{(i)}_{1}+\nu^{(i)}_{2}+\dots+\nu^{(i)}_{k}}}={\rm const}\cdot\frac{\big(|x|q^{k}\big)^{r}}{q^{\nu_{1}^{(i)}+\dots+\nu_{k}^{(i)}}},

for any
z=r\pm\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\ln{q}}
, and any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
The constant above depends on
K
but not on
i
.
Choose
k\in\mathbb{N}
large enough so that
a\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\sup\limits_{x\in K}{|x|}\cdot q^{k}\in(0,1)
.
Since
\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{\nu_{j}^{(i)}}=\nu_{j}
for all
j=1,2,\dots,k
, then
\sup\limits_{i\geq 1}{q^{-\nu_{1}^{(i)}-\nu_{2}^{(i)}-\dots-\nu_{k}^{(i)}}}\leq{\rm const}<\infty
.
Then there exists a constant
c=c_{K}>0
, independent of
i
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{i\geq 1}\sup_{x\in K}\left|x^{z}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\big(q^{-z+\nu_{j}^{(i)}}t^{j};q\big)_{\infty}}{\big(q^{-z+\nu_{j}^{(i)}}t^{j-1};q\big)_{\infty}}}\right|\leq c\cdot a^{r},\qquad\textrm{if}\quad z=r\pm\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\ln{q}}.

Since
\int_{R}^{\infty}{a^{r}{\rm d}r}=-e^{R\ln{a}}/\ln{a}\xrightarrow{R\rightarrow\infty}0
, we have just shown that the contribution of the tails of
\mathcal{C}^{+}
is uniformly small. We can then apply dominated convergence theorem to conclude (
7.20
), as desired.

Step 2.
As in the step above, let
\big\{M^{\nu^{(i)}}\big\}_{i\geq 1}
be a sequence in
\Omega_{q,t}^{{\rm Martin}}
, whose images under
\mathbf{N}
are
\big\{\nu^{(i)}\big\}_{i\geq 1}
.
If the weak limit
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{M^{\nu^{(i)}}}=M
holds, then we show that
M\in\Omega_{q,t}^{{\rm Martin}}
, that there a pointwise limit
\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{\nu^{(i)}}=\nu\in\mathcal{N}
and moreover
M=M^{\nu}
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be arbitrary.
Thanks to Proposition
6.5
, the limit
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{M^{\nu^{(i)}}}=M
implies
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{M_{m}^{\nu^{(i)}}}=M_{m}
.
By Lemma
7.8
(2), there exists
c_{m}\in(0,1)
such that
M_{m}^{\nu^{(i)}}\big(\nu_{m}^{(i)}\geq\cdots\geq\nu_{1}^{(i)}\big)\geq c_{m}>0
for all
i\geq 1
.
Since
M_{m}
is a probability measure, there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
large enough such that
M_{m}((\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m})\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}\colon N_{0}\geq\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\geq-N_{0})>1-\frac{c_{m}}{2}
.
Then
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{M_{m}^{\nu^{(i)}}}=M_{m}
implies
M_{m}^{\nu^{(i)}}((\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m})\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}\colon N_{0}\geq\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\geq-N_{0})>1-c_{m}
for all
i
large enough.
It follows that
N_{0}\geq\nu_{m}^{(i)},\dots,\nu_{1}^{(i)}\geq-N_{0}
for all
i
large enough. In particular, the sequence
\big\{\nu_{m}^{(i)}\big\}_{i\geq 1}
is bounded.

By using the boundedness of the sequences
\big\{\nu^{(i)}_{m}\big\}_{i\geq 1}
and the “diagonal argument” of Proposition
7.4
, there exists a subsequence
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}<\cdots
such that the limits
\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\nu^{(i_{k})}_{m}}
exist, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. In other words, we have the pointwise limit
\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\nu^{(i_{k})}}=\nu
, for some
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
.

We have the obvious implication

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}-\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}{M^{\nu^{(i)}}}=M\Longrightarrow\mathbb{P}-\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}{M^{\nu^{(i_{k})}}}=M,

But step 1 in this proof shows that
\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\nu^{(i_{k})}}=\nu
implies
\mathbb{P}-\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}{M^{\nu^{(i_{k})}}}=M^{\nu}
. By uniqueness of weak limits, we have
M=M^{\nu}
.

We are left to show that
\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}{\nu^{(i)}}=\nu
. Above we only showed the existence of a subsequence
\{i_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
such that
\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\nu^{(i_{k})}}=\nu
. Nevertheless the same argument gives us more. In fact, it shows that any subsequence
\big\{\nu^{(i_{r})}\big\}_{r\geq 1}\subset\big\{\nu^{(i)}\big\}_{i\geq 1}
must have a subsubsequence
\big\{\nu^{(i_{r(s)})}\big\}_{s\geq 1}\subset\big\{\nu^{(i_{r})}\big\}_{r\geq 1}
such that a pointwise limit
\lim\limits_{s\rightarrow\infty}{\nu^{(i_{r(s)})}}=\nu^{\prime}
exists. Then
M=M^{\nu^{\prime}}=M^{\nu}
, but since the map
\mathbf{N}
is bijective, we have
\nu=\nu^{\prime}
. We therefore conclude that the sequence
\big\{\nu^{(i)}\big\}_{i\geq 1}
itself must converge to
\nu
, finishing the proof.
∎

7.3
Relation between the Martin boundary and the (minimal) boundary

The basic relation between the Martin and minimal boundary of the
(q,t)
-GT graph is the following statement, which actually holds in a much greater generality.

Proposition 7.10

(consequence of
[
37
, Theorem 6.1]
)
.

The following inclusion holds
\Omega_{q,t}\subseteq\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
. In other words, for any
M\in\Omega_{q,t}
, there exists a sequence
\{\lambda(N)\}_{N\geq 1}
,
\lambda(N)\in\mathbb{GT}_{N}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}-\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}{M^{\lambda(N)}_{m}}=M_{m}\qquad\forall\,m=0,1,2,\dots.

In many examples, especially in the context of asymptotic representation theory, it is known that the Martin boundary of a branching graph is
equal
to its minimal boundary, e.g.,
[
21
,
37
]
. In our case, we will also prove that this is the case by following the ideas in
[
21
,
41
]
. The following statement, which also holds in greater generality, will be useful.

Proposition 7.11

(consequence of
[
37
, Theorem 9.2]
)
.

Let
M^{\prime}
be any probability measure on
M_{\rm prob}(\mathcal{T})
. There exists a unique Borel probability measure
\pi
belonging to
\Omega_{q,t}
such that

\displaystyle M^{\prime}(S_{\phi})=\int_{M\in\Omega_{q,t}}{M(S_{\phi})\pi({\rm d}M)},

for any finite path
\phi=\big(\phi^{(0)}\prec\phi^{(1)}\prec\cdots\prec\phi^{(n)}\big)
in the
(q,t)
-GT graph.

7.4
Characterization of the boundary of the
\boldsymbol{(q,t)}
-Gelfand–Tsetlin graph

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Let us now prove the items (1), (2) stated in the theorem.

(1) Thanks to Proposition
7.10
, we have
\Omega_{q,t}\subseteq\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}\subseteq M_{\rm prob}(\mathcal{T})
. We claim that
\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}\subseteq\Omega_{q,t}
. Because of Theorem
7.9
, the Martin boundary
\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
(with its induced topology from
M_{\rm prob}(\mathcal{T})
) is homeomorphic to
\mathcal{N}
, under the map
\mathbf{N}
.
The claim would then show that the minimal boundary
\Omega_{q,t}
of the
(q,t)
-GT graph is equal to the Martin boundary
\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
and therefore homeomorphic to
\mathcal{N}
, under the map
\mathbf{N}
.

Let us prove the claim above. Let
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
be arbitrary and let
M^{\nu}\in\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
be the corresponding element of
\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
. Let
\mathfrak{i}\colon\Omega_{q,t}\hookrightarrow\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
be the natural inclusion, considered as a measurable map. By Proposition
7.11
, there exists a unique probability measure
\pi
on
\Omega_{q,t}
such that

\displaystyle M^{\nu}=\int_{M\in\Omega_{q,t}}{M\pi({\rm d}M)}=\int_{M\in\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}}{M(\mathfrak{i}_{*}\pi)({\rm d}M)}.

(7.21)

Note that
i_{*}\pi
is a probability measure on
\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
with
(i_{*}\pi)(\Omega_{q,t})=1
. Let
\widetilde{\pi}
be the pushforward of
\mathfrak{i}_{*}\pi
under the homeomorphism
\mathbf{N}\colon\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
, so
\widetilde{\pi}
is a Borel probability measure on
\mathcal{N}
. Equation (
7.21
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle M^{\nu}=\int_{\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N}}{M^{\widetilde{\nu}}\widetilde{\pi}({\rm d}\widetilde{\nu})}.

(7.22)

We make a subclaim:
\widetilde{\pi}
is the delta mass at
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
. Let us first deduce
\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}\subseteq\Omega_{q,t}
from this latter claim. In fact, if
\widetilde{\pi}
is the delta mass at
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
, then
\mathfrak{i}_{*}\pi
is the delta mass at
M^{\nu}
. But since we had
(i_{*}\pi)(\Omega_{q,t})=1
, then
M^{\nu}\in\Omega_{q,t}
. Since
M^{\nu}
was an arbitrary element of
\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}
, then we conclude
\Omega_{q,t}^{\rm Martin}\subseteq\Omega_{q,t}
.

Let us now prove the subclaim that the probability measure
\widetilde{\pi}
on
\mathcal{N}
satisfying (
7.22
) must be the delta mass at
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
.
We show first that
\widetilde{\pi}
is supported on
\{\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N}\colon\widetilde{\nu}\geq\nu\}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\{\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N}\colon\widetilde{\nu}_{1}\geq\nu_{1},\,\widetilde{\nu}_{2}\geq\nu_{2},\,\widetilde{\nu}_{3}\geq\nu_{3},\dots\}
.
Since
\widetilde{\pi}
is a Borel measure, the opposite would mean the existence of
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\kappa_{1}\leq\kappa_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\kappa_{m}
, such that
\kappa_{i}<\nu_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq m
, and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}\big(\big\{\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N}\colon\widetilde{\nu}_{1}=\kappa_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\nu}_{m}=\kappa_{m}\big\}\big)>0.

As a consequence of (
7.22
) we have, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle M_{m}^{\nu}=\int_{\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N}}{M_{m}^{\widetilde{\nu}}\widetilde{\pi}({\rm d}\widetilde{\nu})}.

(7.23)

We can now apply (
7.23
) to
\kappa=(\kappa_{m}\geq\dots\geq\kappa_{1})\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
:

\displaystyle M_{m}^{\nu}(\kappa)=\int_{\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N}}{M_{m}^{\widetilde{\nu}}(\kappa)\widetilde{\pi}({\rm d}\widetilde{\nu})}.

From Lemma
7.8
(1), the left-hand side of the equality above vanishes, while Lemma
7.8
(2) shows that the right-hand side is at least
c_{m}\cdot\widetilde{\pi}(\{\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N}\colon\widetilde{\nu}_{1}=\kappa_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\nu}_{m}=\kappa_{m}\})>0
, thus there is a contradiction.

From the fact that
\widetilde{\pi}
is supported on
\{\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N}\colon\widetilde{\nu}\geq\nu\}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\{\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N}\colon\widetilde{\nu}_{1}\geq\nu_{1},\widetilde{\nu}_{2}\geq\nu_{2},\dots\}
, and Lemma
7.8
, parts (1) (3), we have that (
7.23
) evaluated at
(\nu_{m}\geq\cdots\geq\nu_{1})
is

\displaystyle M^{\nu}_{m}(\nu_{m}\geq\dots\geq\nu_{1})=\int_{\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N}}{{M_{m}^{\widetilde{\nu}}(\nu_{m}\geq\dots\geq\nu_{1})\widetilde{\pi}({\rm d}\widetilde{\nu})}}=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N}\\
\widetilde{\nu}\geq\nu\end{subarray}}{{M_{m}^{\widetilde{\nu}}(\nu_{m}\geq\dots\geq\nu_{1})\widetilde{\pi}({\rm d}\widetilde{\nu})}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N},\,\widetilde{\nu}\geq\nu\\
\widetilde{\nu}_{i}=\nu_{i}\ \forall i=1,\dots,m\end{subarray}}{{M_{m}^{\widetilde{\nu}}(\nu_{m}\geq\dots\geq\nu_{1})\widetilde{\pi}({\rm d}\widetilde{\nu})}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}+\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N},\,\widetilde{\nu}\geq\nu\\
\widetilde{\nu}_{i}\neq\nu_{i}\textrm{ for some }i\in\{1,\dots,m\}\end{subarray}}{{M_{m}^{\widetilde{\nu}}(\nu_{m}\geq\dots\geq\nu_{1})\widetilde{\pi}({\rm d}\widetilde{\nu})}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N},\widetilde{\nu}\geq\nu\\
\widetilde{\nu}_{i}=\nu_{i}\ \forall\,i=1,\dots,m\end{subarray}}{{M_{m}^{\widetilde{\nu}}(\nu_{m}\geq\dots\geq\nu_{1})\widetilde{\pi}({\rm d}\widetilde{\nu})}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq M_{m}^{\nu}(\nu_{m}\geq\dots\geq\nu_{1})\cdot\widetilde{\pi}(\{\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N}\colon\widetilde{\nu}_{1}=\nu_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\nu}_{m}=\nu_{m}\}).

Next Lemma
7.8
(2) says that
M_{m}^{\nu}(\nu_{m}\geq\dots\geq\nu_{1})\geq c_{m}>0
, so we must have
\widetilde{\pi}(\{\widetilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{N}\colon\widetilde{\nu}_{1}=\nu_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\nu}_{m}=\nu_{m}\})=1
. Since this is true for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows that
\widetilde{\pi}
must be the delta mass at
\nu
, thus proving our second claim and the full characterization of
\Omega_{q,t}
.

Let us return to the statement of item (1) in Theorem
1.3
.
By definition of the map
\mathbf{N}
, the relations (
1.2
) hold.
We have already observed that the Macdonald generating function defining
\mathcal{P}_{M_{m}^{\nu}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
is absolutely convergent on
\mathbb{T}^{m}
.
The last statement that says
M^{\nu}
is determined by the relations (
1.2
) follows from the uniqueness statement in Proposition
7.4
.

(2) Let
\{M_{m}^{\nu}\}_{m\geq 0}
,
\{M_{m}^{A_{k}\nu}\}_{m\geq 0}
, be the
(q,t)
-coherent sequences associated to
M^{\nu}
and
M^{A_{k}\nu}
, respectively.
By Lemma
7.5
, we have the first statement
M^{A_{k}\nu}(S_{A_{k}\phi})=M^{\nu}(S_{\phi})
, for any finite path
\phi
.
Next by virtue of Lemma
6.12
,
M^{A_{k}\nu}_{m}=A_{k}M^{\nu}_{m}
for all
m\geq 0
.
Thus by following the definitions,
M^{A_{k}\nu}_{m}(A_{k}\lambda)=A_{k}M^{\nu}_{m}(A_{k}\lambda)=M^{\nu}_{m}(A_{-k}A_{k}\lambda)=M^{\nu}_{m}(\lambda)
, for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{GT}_{m}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
∎

Appendix A
Basics on
\boldsymbol{q}
-analysis

A good reference for the material on
q
-analysis is
[
2
, Chapter 10]
. Assume
|q|<1
is an arbitrary complex number. Most statements work if
q
is an indeterminate too.

The
q
-numbers
and the
q
-factorial
are defined by

\displaystyle[n]_{q}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}\frac{1-q^{n}}{1-q},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N},

\displaystyle[n]_{q}!\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}[n]_{q}\cdots[2]_{q}[1]_{q},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N},\qquad[0]_{q}!\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}1.

It is evident that
[n]_{q}\rightarrow n
and
[n]_{q}!\rightarrow n!
, as
q\rightarrow 1
, for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Observe that we can also define
[x]_{q}
for any
x\in\mathbb{C}
, as before, and it also holds that
[x]_{q}\rightarrow x
as
q\rightarrow 1
. The
q
-Gamma function
is defined by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{q}(z)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}}(1-q)^{1-z}\frac{(q;q)_{\infty}}{(q^{z};q)_{\infty}}.

From the definition, the
q
-functional equation

\displaystyle\Gamma_{q}(z+1)=[z]_{q}\Gamma_{q}(z),\qquad z\notin\{\dots,-2,-1,0\},

is evident. The
q
-Gamma function is a meromorphic function with simple poles at
z=0,-1,-2,\dots
and all their shifts by an integral multiple of
2\pi\sqrt{-1}/\ln{q}
.
The
q
-Gamma function has no zeroes in
\mathbb{C}
. Moreover, we have the following convergence to the Gamma function.

Theorem A.1

(
[
2
, Corollary 10.3.4]
)
.

For any
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\dots,-2,-1,0\}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{q\rightarrow 1}{\Gamma_{q}(z)}=\Gamma(z).

Remark A.2
.

As a consequence of Stieltjes–Vitali theorem, the convergence
\lim\limits_{q\rightarrow 1}{\Gamma_{q}(z)}=\Gamma(z)
holds uniformly on compact subsets of
